| 10 | Stale renders | The bridge cannot render Excalidraw, so a pin makes the render stale: `stale_pins` lists the pins since the render, in the event and the state, and the room's board shows them on a sticky note in the corner ("New pins · open the board to place them") until a client opens the board, which lays them out and saves a fresh render at once. The ticket's "the PNG is re-rendered the next time a client has the board open". |
| 11 | Who may pin | `POST /whiteboard/{floor}/pin` is on the bridge's socket only (the office CLI runs on that host, M14); the helper does not proxy it. A note can also be pinned with the WebSocket command `whiteboard.pin`. Both are audited without the note's text. |
| 12 | Lazy loading | `game/src/whiteboard/load.ts` is the only door: one `import('./editor')` on the first open, React 19, ReactDOM and Excalidraw (and its CSS) behind it, mounted with `createElement` (no JSX transform). `tools/check-bundle.ts` runs after every `pnpm build` and fails it when the entry's static import graph (from the Vite manifest; dynamic imports excluded) or its CSS contains React, ReactDOM or Excalidraw, by module and by markers in the built code, or when `index.html` preloads an editor chunk. |
| 13 | No CDN | All 234 font files Excalidraw packages (Xiaolai's 209 CJK subsets included) are copied into the build at `assets/excalidraw-0.18.1/fonts/` (a Vite plugin; the dev server serves them from `node_modules`) and `EXCALIDRAW_ASSET_PATH` points there before the import. Excalidraw still lists esm.sh as a fallback; with every face present it never gets there, and the e2e spec blocks and lists every request to another host than the game's (none). +12.5 MiB on disk (and in the helper binary that embeds the game); a face loads only when its unicode range is used. |
| 14 | What phones home, off | Our own `MainMenu` (canvas background, search) and `WelcomeScreen` (one line, no links), `aiEnabled: false`, `validateEmbeddable: () => false` (no web embeds), canvas actions export/load/save/theme/clear off (our Clear asks first and keeps the history), links on the board are not followed (a toast says so), the library sidebar's trigger and the help button hidden (their links go to excalidraw.com, GitHub, Discord; CSS in the lazy chunk). No collaboration is configured, so there is no socket. |
| 15 | Esc | Excalidraw first (finishing a text or a line, closing a menu, a popup, a dialog, the sidebar, a context menu, the link editor: `isIdle(state, true)`), then our picker or confirmation, then the selection is dropped, then a drawing tool goes back to the pointer, then the board closes. The close button always closes, unless the changes cannot be saved (row 21). Closing saves what is left (bounded: 8 s). |
| 16 | Game input while open | The overlay is a fixed layer above the HUD (outside `.hud`: its `button` rules must not reach Excalidraw's), the walker is released and stopped on open, `App.onKey` returns at once while it is open and `dialogOpen` includes it (the frame loop's `blocked`). Not a modal `<dialog>`: Excalidraw's popovers must stay interactive. |
| 17 | The room's board | One texture per floor (`render/whiteboardTexture.ts`): `render.png?v=<render_version>` contained on white at the board's 3.2 × 1.3 m (2048 × 832), the stale pins' note when stale, the placeholder ("Empty · look here and press E to draw") when there is no render; loaded again only when the state changes, the previous one disposed. |
| 18 | Recent drops | `drops.list{limit}` (default 20, at most 50): Drop Zone's data directory (`--dropzone-data`) and its month folders one level down, newest by mtime, read only through imgcache's allowlist (a new `SniffPath` reads only the first bytes). Non-images are **listed greyed** with their reason, so a drop that is "missing" from the picker is explained; only images can be pinned. `GET /drops/file` serves bytes or a 480 px thumbnail (two decodes at a time). Kept generic for #13's composer. |
| 19 | Fonts' licences | Not all OFL: Comic Shanns is MIT, Liberation Sans is the old 1.05 (GPLv2 with the font exception; its licence agreement and the GPLv2 ship in full), Cascadia is Microsoft's OFL-based licence (the font's own name-table text and the repository's). `src/fonts/excalidraw/*/LICENSE` carry each font's copyright as its files state it; the credits panel lists them and `FONT_LICENSES.txt` carries them, with Excalidraw's MIT notice. Replacing the old Liberation with a 2.x OFL one is left for later (it changes text metrics). |
| 20 | head.json stays small | The head holds only bounded things: the 20 kept versions without their files, the newest 100 stale pins (the room's note and the editor show a few; `stale` is by version, so it stays right), the last 64 pin request ids. A commit marshals the head first and refuses (`413`) one over 1 MiB before it writes anything, so the store never writes a head it would not read. |
| 21 | A close that cannot save | Never drops the only copy. The close drains (each save takes what was drawn during the one before) within 8 s; if a save fails or the time runs out, the board stays open and asks: Retry, Close and keep a draft, or Discard changes (an explicit choice). A draft is kept in memory for the page's life and in `localStorage` when its JSON is at most 4 M characters (wrapped in try/catch: blocked or full storage leaves the memory copy), keyed by the bridge's host and path (never the token) and the floor. The next open reconciles it into the board with `reconcileElements` (its edits have higher versions; pins since stay) and saves it. **A save that lands drops the draft only if it has all of it** (every draft element is in the saved scene at a version Excalidraw's reconcile keeps over the draft's, and the draft's appState, if it changed one, is the saved one; memory and stored copies are checked on their own), and **a closed session never touches a draft**: a save still in flight when the user chose "Close, keep a draft" must not delete the newer draft that close kept (fix round 3). A closed session posts nothing more (a save waiting on its files or its PNG stops), so Discard cannot be saved behind the user; a request already on the wire cannot be recalled and may still land. A background retry that saves while the choice is up closes the board, as was asked. |
| 22 | One snapshot per save | A save's elements, files, appState and base are captured once (after the files are re-filed by hash); the files go up from it, the PNG is exported from it, and it is posted on its base. A merge (an agent's pin, another window) during the upload or the export moves the base: the round starts over instead of posting, since a stale scene on the new base would pass the bridge's check and drop what the merge brought. The board's appState is part of what is saved (a background change alone saves) and of the merge (remote wins unless changed here since the last save). **Merges may overlap** (an update arrives while one waits on image bytes) and a save may land during one: each merge checks its board's version against the session's base after every await and drops a board older than that before it touches the scene, the appState, the base or the saved signatures, so the base only goes up and an older merge finishing last cannot bring back an older background as "saved" (fix round 3). Not serialized: a newer update does not wait on an older one's image download. Every other async step that writes after an await (re-filing images by hash, laying out agents' pins at open, pinning a drop) re-reads the live scene and checks the session is still open first. |
| 23 | The game behind the overlay | While the whiteboard is open, everything around it up to the body is `inert` (focus and pointer stay in it; it is a div, not a modal `<dialog>`, so it does this itself). Excalidraw's popovers are added to the body after it opens and are not touched. The limits plate's observer counts the overlay's `open` as a screen dialog's, so it steps aside at once. |

## 2026-10-01 — #15 (M13): the screen-share TV

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The transport of a frame: `screen.frame{req_id, jpeg}` over `/ws` as the ticket says, or HTTP? | **The JPEG goes over HTTP**, `POST /screen/frame/{req_id}` with the raw bytes, through the helper to the bridge (the approved deviation, the same reasoning as #51's row 1): a client message is capped at 256 KiB (`maxClientMsg`) and a 1920 px JPEG at quality 0.8 can be several hundred KiB, more in base64. `screen.frame` stays a command, for the answer that has **no** frame (`{req_id, error, message}`), so a grab fails at once with the game's reason instead of timing out. `screen.frame_request` and `screen.grabbed` are connection messages to the sharing game (no `seq`, never replayed: a replayed frame request would make a game upload for a grab that is long gone). |
| 2 | Who may post a frame? | Only an answer to a request: the bridge accepts `POST /screen/frame/{id}` only while a grab waits for that id, and the id is 128 random bits sent to the sharing game's connection only. Behind the helper the route has the `/upload` guards (Host, token, Origin when present). The bridge checks what arrives (magic bytes, 8 MiB on the bytes read, at most 1920 × 4096 px from the header before decoding, then a full decode) and a refusal fails the grab with the same code. |
| 3 | Which client is "the game"? | A connection that sent `screen.share` (the game sends it after every connect, sharing or not). `no_client` means no such connection (the CLI asking is a client too, so "any client" would never be empty); `not_sharing` means games are connected but none shares. With several sharing games, the one that **started** last is asked; `screen.state.surface` is that one's. A disconnect is "not sharing" and fails its waiting grabs at once. |
| 4 | Timeout | **5 s** for the whole grab (`DefaultScreenTimeout`, `Options.ScreenTimeout`): the target is under 2 s (the e2e test asserts a 1280×720 grab under 2 s on llvmpipe; the Mac is to be measured), and past 5 s something is wrong (a hidden tab whose timers are throttled, a dead tunnel). At most 4 grabs wait at once (`busy` beyond, not queued); the game answers them one at a time. |
| 5 | Where frames go | `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/herdr-world/screen` (tmpfs), `0700`, refused unless it is a directory owned by the bridge's user; one `0600` file per grab, written under a temporary name and renamed, named `frame-<unix ms>-<req_id>.jpg`. The TTL (10 min) is read from the name on the hub's clock (`Options.Now`), so it is tested with a fake clock; the janitor sweeps every 30 s and the bridge sweeps at start (also a half-written temporary file). Without `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`: `/run/user/<uid>` when it exists (logged), else the bridge starts and every grab says how to fix it; frames never fall back to a disk-backed `/tmp`. `--screen-dir` overrides. |
| 6 | Audit and toast | Every grab writes one `screen.grab` row, refused or not, with the caller's client name, the floor, the requester and the frame's size (never the bytes or the picture). `screen.share` is audited like any command (each start and stop). The requester is shown to the user, so it is validated: 1–64 bytes of UTF-8, no control or format characters (no bidi overrides), no U+FFFD (what JSON makes of invalid UTF-8), no outer spaces. |
| 7 | The picker | Chrome's own, like Google Meet (the owner's ask: "share a window and the screen, using the browser's own capability; a single tab would be awesome too"): no `displaySurface` hint, `selfBrowserSurface: exclude`, `surfaceSwitching: include`, `monitorTypeSurfaces: include`, no audio (the TV and the grabs are visual), ≤ 1920 × 1080 at 15 fps ideal / 30 max (bounds on what Chrome sends, not a filter on what can be picked; `min`/`exact` are never used, they reject before the picker), and a fresh `CaptureController` with `no-focus-change` set **before** the call, feature-detected. `getDisplayMedia` is the first thing the click does (user activation). Research: `docs/research/screen-share.md`. |
| 8 | Cancel vs. macOS denial | Both are `NotAllowedError` and there is no standard way to tell them apart. A cancel must say nothing and a denial must say where to fix it, so: Chrome's "Permission denied **by system**" message (not localised), or a `NotAllowedError` on a Mac that comes back within 250 ms (faster than a person can see a picker and cancel it), is the OS; every other `NotAllowedError` is a cancel. The help names System Settings → Privacy & Security → Screen & System Audio Recording. Other errors have their own words (`NotReadableError`: the source could not be captured; `InvalidStateError`: click again). |
| 9 | The TV picture | A `VideoTexture` of the capture's `<video>` (a hidden 1 px element in the page: a detached one may stop delivering frames), sRGB, on an unlit `MeshBasicMaterial` with `toneMapped: false`, letterboxed on a black screen (never cropped or stretched), one texture for the game that every floor's TV borrows (a floor disposing never disposes it). A new size makes a new texture (three.js keeps the first upload's size). **three r186 bug worked around:** the WebGL2 backend keys a render pipeline by a map's `mapping` only, so the TV picture reused the program of the sign beside it, which lacks the shader-side sRGB decode a video needs (it is uploaded raw), and showed washed out (#164b9b as #5394cd); the picture's material gets a `customProgramCacheKey` of its own. The e2e test checks the colour through the TV. |
| 10 | Mute, stop, surface switch | A muted track shows "Paused" on the TV and refuses frames (`unavailable`): stale imagery is never sent when the browser says the source is unavailable; minimise does not always mute, which is not claimed. Stop runs the cleanup itself (`track.stop()` fires no `ended`) and is idempotent against Chrome's own "Stop sharing"; a capture generation guards everything asynchronous (a late picker answer, an encode during a Stop). The surface is read again on `resize`, `unmute` and each frame request, so "Share this tab instead" shows in the badge and `screen.state`. |
| 11 | The badge | In the top bar, before About, in every mode (a share is the game's, not a floor's): off, a plain "Share screen" button; sharing, a red label-maker tape **LIVE** with a breathing dot (still under reduced motion), what is shared, and Stop. On a top bar narrower than 1500 px the words go ("Share screen" becomes its icon; the tape and Stop stay), and so does the connection badge's word (its dot and title stay): the top bar had 2 px to spare at 1280, and the counts keep their words there and the floor's name stays whole at 1024 (`e2e/m3b.spec.ts`). The TV's look-at bar says the same ("Live · sharing a window · agents see a frame only when they ask"). Chrome's own Stop says "Screen sharing stopped"; the user's Stop says nothing. |
| 12 | For M14 (#16) | The CLI sends `screen.grab {floor, requester}` on the bridge socket (`herdr-world cmd` works today); Go has `(*proto.Conn).ScreenGrab` (send, wait for the ack, a refusal as `*proto.ScreenGrabError` with its code) and `(*server.Hub).GrabScreen` in-process. |
| 13 | A frame request that outlives its capture (review round 1) | Bound to the capture generation that was live **when it arrived**, not when its turn comes: a request queued behind a slow upload is dropped when that capture ends (the controller empties the queue on Stop or Chrome's Stop), and the answer refuses one whose generation is not the live one, so a request never encodes a later Share of another source. Dropped requests are not refused again over `/ws`: the bridge already failed them on `screen.share {sharing:false}` (or the disconnect). An upload already on its way at the Stop carries the stopped capture's frame and goes on (the bridge refuses it, as no grab waits any more). |
| 14 | A grab abandoned while being registered (review round 1) | `query` returns on the caller's context while the registration is still queued for the hub goroutine, so the registration checks that context first and asks nothing when it ended; and each frame request carries its own `time.AfterFunc` deadline (`ScreenTimeout`) that ends it with `frame_timeout` on the hub goroutine whoever waits, so a request registered just before its caller left still frees its slot (4 such orphans used to make every later grab `busy`). The caller's own timer stays: it answers on time even when the hub goroutine is slow. |
| 15 | The macOS denial under the plan limits panel (UX review) | That help is long and wraps to the width of the screen, so the limits panel (top right, top layer) covered its end at 1280 and 1920. A toast the panel would cover goes just under the panel (`Hud.placeToast`, measured when shown); short toasts are not moved. Narrowing every toast instead could not clear a ~500 px panel at 1280 without a column too narrow to read. |

## 2026-10-01 — #52: dragging issue cards between status columns

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What order does a column have? | **The GitHub project's own item order (option A).** `ProjectV2.items(orderBy: {field: POSITION, direction: ASC})` (POSITION is the only field, and the default; the feed names it anyway) is one manual order across all statuses; filtered per Status it keeps its relative order, which is the column order of an unsorted board view on github.com. It survives restarts and other clients, and another client's reorder shows on the next poll. The bridge's SQLite (option B) would have drifted from GitHub's board; columns without an order (C) do not meet the issue. A view with its own sort shows GitHub's sort there, not this order: only unsorted views agree. |
| 2 | How it is read | In the feed itself: the project's first 100 items (their id, the Issue's node id, the Status option) cost no extra point in the spike's measurement (9 → 9); more are paged 100 at a time, at most 5 pages. Each card gets `position`, its 0-based rank among the cards (matched by the Issue's node id, never its number: another repository's issue may have it); drafts, pull requests, content out of reach and issues that are not cards are skipped but stay in the provider's full sequence (the top of a column needs it). Past the page limit no card has a position (never a partial order) and the board is `truncated`. The default `archivedStates` leaves archived items out, like the Status the feed already reads. |
| 3 | How the client sorts | `columnViews` sorts every column by `position`, done columns too; cards without one follow, by the old rule (urgency then recency; done: latest closed). A provider with no order sends no position and gets exactly the old sort. The 3D wall uses the same views, so it follows the order (it stays read-only). |
| 4 | Wire shape | **`issue.set_status` gets an optional `after`** (protocol v1, additive): absent is the old status-only write (the modal's Move to… and older clients); `null` is the top of the column; a card id is right after that card. Go tells absent from null with `proto.Optional`. It is validated like an id, must be another card of the target column on the last board, else `bad_request`. A new capability `order` (GitHub: with a project, like `set_status`) gates it: an `after` without it is `unsupported`. A board with `set_status` but no `order` still takes drops between columns, with a column highlight only and no `after`. The tracker interface: `SetStatus(ctx, id, column, *Anchor)`, nil for the status only. No GitHub id goes on the wire. |
| 5 | The GitHub writes | Resolve or add the project item (as before), then the Status write **only when it changes** (a reorder in the column writes the place only; without `after` the status is always written, as before), then `updateProjectV2ItemPosition {projectId, itemId, afterId}`. `afterId`: the anchor card's item; for the top of a non-empty column the item right before the column's first card in the global sequence, the moved item left out (none when that card is the project's first); for an empty column none (the top of the project). It is worked out before anything is written, from the last feed's order when nothing was written since, else from a fresh read of the items (about 1 point a page). A failed write after one that landed (the place after the status, the status after an add) says so ("its status changed, but its place in the column did not") and is a `provider.ErrPartial` besides its own kind: the bridge, which knows no provider, refreshes after it as after a success and drops the polls in flight, so the board shows GitHub's truth at once (fix round 2). An anchor that is no longer in the order is `stale`. Tests use fakes only; no mutation ran against the real project. |
| 6 | A drop into Done | **Changes the status only; the issue stays open**, the same as Move to… (closing is `issue.close`, with its confirmation and reason). Done sorts by position like every column. |
| 7 | A move right after a poll | Bridge: every successful write counts on the floor; a poll that began before the latest write is dropped when it lands (not sent, not cached) and the refresh runs instead. Before, a refresh asked for while a poll was in flight was lost (the poll's end set the next poll a minute later) and the in-flight poll's older board was shown. After a successful `issue.set_status` the bridge's last board also has the card in its new column, so a second drop anchored on it is valid before the refresh lands. Game: a drop shows at once; a failure puts it back and says `failureText('issue.set_status', …)` in the board itself, in a `role="alert"` line above the columns (a HUD toast would be under the modal board and outside its accessibility tree) until it is dismissed or the next drag starts; after a partial failure the refresh that follows shows the card's real column (the failed move is forgotten, so nothing holds it back); an acknowledged move holds until a non-initial `board.issues` that came after the ack shows it in that slot, or 20 s at most, so a stale board never makes the card jump back. Drops are sent one after the other (a drop may be anchored on the card the one before moved). |
| 8 | A poll during a drag | The overlay does not redraw the columns while a drag is live (the drag measures them); what came meanwhile is drawn when it ends. |
| 9 | Interaction | Pointer events (mouse, pen, touch), not HTML5 drag and drop (no touch). Mouse and pen: the drag starts after 5 px, so a click still opens the card (and the click after a drag is swallowed: a drag ended while the button is still down, by Esc or the columns going away, swallows the click of its release, however long that takes up to 10 s; a new press ends that early, and a keyboard click is never swallowed). Touch: a 380 ms long press, so a swipe on a card still scrolls the board; once live, a non-passive `touchmove` on the board stops the page from scrolling. The note lifts (tilted, a pinhole where its pin was) and follows the pointer; its old spot is a dashed ghost that keeps the pin; the column under the pointer lights up; the slot is a sun-yellow thread with a pin at each end, drawn above the note (the slot is usually under it), placed by each card's midpoint. Near the board's edges it scrolls (sideways and down). Esc, a drop outside a column, on "No status" (a source only) or on the card's own place, and a cancelled pointer send nothing. `prefers-reduced-motion`: no lift animation and no glide of the line; the line still shows. The modal's Move to… stays the keyboard and screen-reader path; a keyboard drag is not in this change. |

## 2026-10-01 — #91: the Settings window

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Where do the changeable things live? | **A Settings window of its own** (`ui/settings.ts`), opened by a gear next to the info button (screen share sits before both) or by `,` (free; ignored while a field has the keys, and while any dialog is open). About (`ui/about.ts`) is read-only again: the build versions, the mismatch note and "Settings moved to ⚙". The plan-limits switch (Display) and the day-and-night fields moved as they were: the same `localStorage` keys (`h3d.limits.hidden`, `h3d.time.zone`, `h3d.time.lat`), no migration. |
| 2 | Backdrop | **`none`** (#51): it holds typed fields, so a stray click must not drop a half-typed one. The close button and Esc close it, like About. |
| 3 | How #90 and later features plug in | **A section registry**: `addSection(id, title, render, { store })`. The dialog owns each section's `h3` and a "where is this kept" tag, **This browser** (`localStorage` prefs) or **Bridge** (shared by every client); `store` is required, so no section goes unlabelled. `render(body)` fills the body once, and may return `{ open, dispose }`. An id added twice throws. Sections keep the order they were added in. |
| 4 | Top bar room | The gear is one more 34 px button in a bar that had 2 px to spare at 1280 and a floor name that just fits at 1024. Under 1500 px the actions get a 4 px gap (6 px above) and the word buttons 9 px of padding (12 px above), which gives the room back: the counts keep their words at 1280 (`e2e/m3b.spec.ts`). |

## 2026-10-01 — #95: prompts to Claude are typed

`herdr agent prompt` sends a bracketed paste. Claude Code v2.1.287 wraps a paste of 20 or more characters in
`<pasted_content>`, tells the model it may hold instructions the user did not write, and never parses a slash command
inside it: a hired orchestrator asked to confirm `/write-code`, and the skill was not loaded. Measured end to end in
the issue (real panes, the agents' own transcripts compared byte for byte).

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which agents | **Claude only** (`agent_kind` `claude`; the hire's harness). Codex keeps a paste as typed text (2 KiB multi-line checked exact), so it keeps `agent prompt` and its rules. |
| 2 | How | `Control.TypePrompt`: one `pane send-text` per chunk of at most **700 characters** (by rune, never inside a UTF-8 sequence), **50 ms** apart, then `send-keys enter`. Claude Code takes an input burst of more than 800 characters as a paste; chunks with no gap merge into such bursts; 10 ms already kept them apart, so 700 / 50 ms is the margin. `TypeGap` (default 50 ms) is a `Control` field so tests run without it. |
| 3 | Allowlist | One new op, `type-text`: the **same command** as `send-text` (`pane send-text <pane> <text>`), but its placeholder `{chunk}` allows `\n` (a new line in Claude's input, not Enter) and caps one call at 700 characters. `send-text`'s `ValidInputText` (no control characters, 8 KiB) stays for the laptop's keys. Enter is the existing `key-enter`. `chunk` is a secret placeholder: never quoted in an error. Every chunk is validated before the first is typed. |
| 4 | What is typed | Normalized: `\r\n` and a lone `\r` as `\n` (a typed CR is Enter: one CRLF prompt became three messages), a tab as four spaces (Claude drops a typed tab). Any other control character (ESC, NUL, DEL, C1…) is refused, at the command (`bad_request` before anything runs, Claude workers only) and again in `Control`. Blank text too. |
| 5 | Caps | **Unchanged**: 16 KiB for `worker.prompt`, an answered ask's prompt and the wrap-up (with its footer), 32 KiB for a hire's first prompt, now measured **as typed** (four bytes a tab) for Claude, so an over-cap prompt is a clear `bad_request`. At ~80 ms per KiB that is ~1.3 s and ~2.6 s of typing, inside the game's 20 s `worker.prompt` and 90 s hire timeouts (ready 5 s + typing + ack 5 s + retry 5 s for a prompt). 64 KiB typed exact in the issue's checks, but no field needs it; `Control` refuses anything over 32 KiB as typed. |
| 6 | When | Text typed before Claude's input box is up is lost, so the bridge waits for herdr to say `idle` or `done` (the hire: within the trust timeout; a laptop prompt: `PromptReadyWait`, 5 s, for a state herdr does not know yet). To a `working` Claude it is typed and Enter pressed at once: Claude queues it (`queued: true`). A `blocked` worker, or one with an ask, is refused as before, except the prompt after a cancelled ask, which waits for the dialog's `blocked` to go. |
| 7 | Started? | `agent prompt --wait` did this before. Now the hub watches herdr's status for `PromptAckWait` (5 s): `working` / `blocked`, or a start it saw (a counter per worker, so a turn shorter than the 50 ms poll still counts). Not started: Enter again, as for a stalled paste (the hire: up to 3 times; just past the trust dialog the first Enter is dropped about half the time with either path). |
| 8 | Interleaving | A chunked prompt is many herdr calls: it holds the worker's input lane throughout. The laptop's prompts already ran there; the hire's first prompt now does too (`onLane`), so a laptop key cannot land between two chunks. |
| 9 | Regression check | The 800-character limit is Claude Code's and may change. The usage tracker reads every Claude transcript line; a person's prompt (or queued prompt) with a `<pasted_content>` wrapper whose words hash to a prompt the bridge typed into that worker in the last 10 minutes is logged as a warning (only hashes are kept, never the text). `unwrapPasted` stays for the transcript view: a person can still paste in the terminal. |
| 10 | Typed, so typing rules apply | A typed prompt is the user's own words: a leading `/command` runs (the point). Claude Code's other first-character modes apply too (`!` is its shell mode), as for a person at the keyboard. |
| 11 | A prompt that fails part way (review R1) | Once its first chunk is sent, any failure of `TypePrompt` (a chunk, its context, the Enter) is a `PartlyTypedError`: Claude's input box may hold a prefix, and a retry (the game offers one after `prompt_failed`) or any later prompt would be appended to it and sent with it. The hub **erases it at once, on the worker's input lane** (before any other input there), with a context of its own (a prompt cut short by its context, or by the bridge stopping, is erased too), for the laptop's prompts (and so the ask's and the wrap-up's) and the hire's. **How**: `ctrl+u backspace`, repeated, through the laptops' existing `send-keys` shape (no new op; 64 keys a call), then a pause for Claude to work through them (500 ms + 0.5 ms a pair). Measured on v2.1.287 in a throwaway session: `ctrl+u` deletes to the start of the *visual* line (wrap-aware), so a pair takes a visual line and at least one character; the count is one per new line plus one per 10 characters, never more than the characters (a pane narrower than 20 columns of wide characters could leave some). Extra pairs on an empty box do nothing, and neither key interrupts a working agent, exits or opens a dialog. Rejected: `ctrl+c` (it clears an idle box but arms "Press Ctrl-C again to exit"; a working agent is interrupted and keeps the text, which a later turn then sent), `Esc Esc` (interrupts a working agent; on an empty box opens the rewind menu; an open autocomplete takes one), `ctrl+l` (`chat:clearInput` in the keybinding docs, clears nothing on v2.1.287), backspaces alone (7000 characters took 12 s; the pairs take < 1 s for 16 KiB), `ctrl+g` with a no-op `$EDITOR` (the editor is each Claude's own environment: a person's vim on workers the bridge did not start, and a person pressing ctrl+g at the laptop would lose their text). Live: a 4-chunk multi-line prompt failing at its 3rd chunk, erased (242 pairs, 0.65 s), then a 1385-character multi-line prompt typed: exact in the transcript, no prefix, not a paste, to an idle and to a working Claude (queued, the turn not interrupted). What precedes the prompt in the box (if anything) may be erased too: it would have been sent with it. **Erase fails too**: the worker's input is marked dirty (in memory); its next prompt erases first and is refused (`prompt_failed`, nothing typed) while that fails. Errors and logs never quote the text. |


## 2026-10-01 — #92: AI integration settings

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Where | **`internal/ai`**, stdlib only (`TestAIIsFeatureAgnostic` lists its imports): the `Classifier` and `LLM` interfaces, `FakeClassifier` / `FakeLLM`, the clients, the shared bounds and the role state machine. The hub, `proto` and `app` carry its settings; a feature (#90) takes `Service.Classifier()` / `Service.LLM()`. |
| 2 | Where are keys | **One `0600` file `ai.json` next to `world.db`** (settings and keys of both roles, atomic write, directory `0700`), not in SQLite, which other tools read. `--ai-file` / `HERDR_WORLD_AI_FILE` moves it. A malformed file stops the bridge instead of being overwritten. The five `HERDR_WORLD_AI_*` overrides win per field; their keys are **files**, so a key is never in the environment, and the panel shows them read-only. |
| 3 | A key is never sent back | `View` has `key_set` and `key_hint` (last 4, only for a key of 8+ characters). Errors are built from clipped provider text run through `ai.Scrub` (the key, `Bearer …`, key-shaped strings); the audit row of `ai.*` is `{role, base_url, model, key_given, extra_body}`. Tested on the wire and in the audit log. |
| 4 | "Test before save" | The bridge enforces it: `ai.test` records a fingerprint of the whole config (key included); `ai.set` is `test_required` unless its resulting config matches a pass of the last 30 minutes. The game also keeps Save disabled until a pass for exactly the fields it holds. A restart keeps a saved role `ready` (it passed when it was saved), with no call. |
| 5 | What a failed test does | Only a test of the config **in use** changes the role's state (to `invalid`; a pass lifts it). A draft with a wrong key typed in the panel must not pause a model features are using. `ai.models` never changes a state. |
| 6 | LLM without a key | Allowed: Ollama and vLLM run without one and the ticket names them. The LLM is `incomplete` without a base URL or a model only. The classifier needs both a key and a model. |
| 7 | Retries | At most one per call, including the `response_format` fallback (a 400 with `response_format` is repeated without, and `json_mode` is remembered only if that works). 401/403 and 429 are never retried. The timeout is per attempt. A waiter for one of the two slots leaves with its context and holds nothing; after a slot is won the role is checked again, so calls queued behind a rejected key make no request. |
| 8 | Notifying clients | One `ai.update` event (the whole state, flat like `limits.update`, in the replay ring) whenever the service says something changed; the snapshot has `ai`. The game toasts on a `ready → invalid` transition caused by a feature's call (`last_error.runtime`) and never on a snapshot. |
| 9 | Fixture bridge | The e2e's simulated bridge (`tools/fixture-ai.ts`) implements the same rules in TypeScript and calls fake Jev and OpenAI servers started by the spec (`simjev=<url>`); it does not run the Go bridge. The Go rules are pinned by `internal/ai` and `internal/server` tests against `httptest` servers. |
| 10 | Review round 2 | Keys of any length are scrubbed (rejecting short keys would not cover a key file). Model ids and descriptions are scrubbed of the key only, not of key-shaped strings: a 32+ character model id is legitimate. A passing test covers the submitted **and** the resolved config (a bare URL tested first, then the resolved one submitted). The environment's LLM base URL is remembered as resolved (`env_base` in `ai.json`, keyed by the environment URL, kept by `ai.clear`). Tests and loads take the global slots; a test is bounded per request by its role's timeout; a runtime 401 counts only against the config the request used. The command log of the game redacts `args.key`. |

## 2026-10-02 — #90: follow-up questions in an agent's final message

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Who is looked at | Regular workers and orchestrators of Claude or Codex. **Freelancers are skipped** (`world.Freelancer`): their questions are for the orchestrator, and a need for a human comes through the report's `input_required`. A pending `ask` or a `blocked` status is skipped too: those have their own card. |
| 2 | Off means off | The pipeline runs only with the feature's own toggle on **and** both models `ready`; otherwise `Service.Detect` returns `ai.ErrNotReady` before any call. Configuring the AI integration never switches it on. The env override (`HERDR_WORLD_FOLLOWUPS_ENABLED`) is for headless setups and may switch it on without a test; it still needs both models ready. |
| 3 | Test before enabling | Like `ai.set`: `followups.test` remembers the fingerprints of both models' configs (`ai.Service.Fingerprint`, a hash that never reveals the key) and `followups.set {enabled: true}` is `test_required` unless a pass of the last 30 minutes covers the configs in use. A test that ran but scored a sample on the wrong side answers `ok: false` with the scores (the panel shows them); a failed call is an error. |
| 4 | One parse per line | `transcript.TurnEvents` reads a line once into turn events (prompt, interrupted, tool result, assistant part, turn done / complete); `WrapUpTracker` (#83) and `FollowupTracker` both apply them (`txLines` parses once when either is there), sharing `lastMessage` (Claude's message-id grouping, Codex's per-message text and `last_agent_message`). The wrap-up fixtures stay green; `TestTurnEventsShared` pins that `Line` and `Events` agree. |
| 5 | Turn end | Claude: the final message's `stop_reason` (not `tool_use`) or `system/turn_duration`, whichever first; Codex: `task_complete`. Interrupted turns have none. The hub then waits for 2 s of quiet and herdr not `working` (re-checked every usage round, ~2 s), as the wrap-up does. |
| 6 | What is sent | The final assistant message only, its last 96,000 characters (~24k tokens at 4 characters a token, approximated by characters, never counted) to the classifier, the same tail to the LLM when the score reaches the threshold. Never the transcript or tool output. |
| 7 | Strict validation | 1–8 questions of 1–300 characters, known kinds, a choice with 2–8 options of 1–120 characters: anything else rejects the whole list (the fallback item, with the message's tail), so a bad extraction is never half shown. Options on `yes_no` / `open` are dropped rather than rejecting (models add Yes / No). |
| 8 | Once per message | `followup_seen` (SQLite) by worker and the message's SHA-256: a restart or re-read never classifies it again. The open follow-up is stored with it, so a restart shows it again until it is answered or cleared; clearing marks the row. Only the last 20 messages per worker are kept. A classifier failure is not recorded, so a later restart may retry; an ended turn seen while the feature was off is not queued for later. `turn_id` is the message's hash prefix: the same message is the same turn. |
| 9 | Answering | One prompt through `worker.prompt`'s lane (typed for Claude, #95), checked for staleness again on the lane, and the follow-up cleared once it went. Unanswered questions are left out (numbered as asked). A fallback's free answer goes as it is. The game's card keeps the prompt box on: typing a prompt is answering too, and clears it. |
| 10 | Card look | The ask card's (#57) layout and tokens, drawn with a dashed border and a "Detected automatically" chip (its title gives the score): the bridge inferred it, the agent waits on no dialog. It hides behind an ask's card. |
| 11 | Gate wording | "Does the message ask the user one or more questions…" scored closing offers ("Say if you want those run too") 0.87–0.89, over the 0.7 threshold, so they were flagged. Reworded to ask for a direct question or decision the agent needs answered, and to call "say if you want X" / "let me know if…" an optional offer. Calibrated live against `jev-latest`: offers 0.11–0.13, real questions 0.89–0.98, a plain message 0.03. Topic-style bullets under "Open questions:" score 0.74, close to the threshold. |

## 2026-10-02 — #98: the desk monitor's activity card (game side)

- **One draw for every card on a floor.** The card (mascot, word, detail, counter) is one quad per desk in one
  `InstancedMesh`. The mascots are one sprite sheet (crab and cloud × 14 poses × 6 frames of 32 px pixel art, drawn
  once at load); the frame comes from a time uniform, the pose row and the desk's phase from instance attributes, so no
  canvas is drawn while they animate. Reduced motion sets the uniform's multiplier to 0 (frame 0).
- **The elapsed counter is glyphs, not text drawn per desk.** A static atlas of 16 glyphs (digits, `:`, `h`, `m`, `d`,
  a clock); each card's 8 slots are glyph indexes in two vec4 attributes, written once a second (a few hundred bytes for
  a whole floor). Redrawing the text cells each second instead would have meant a canvas draw and a texture upload of
  the whole atlas per second for every visible floor; the shader digits keep every desk's counter live at no canvas
  cost, and the format (`0:07`, `1h 05m`, `3d 04h`) stays a pure, tested function.
- **The word and the detail are a text-atlas cell per desk**, drawn only when what they say changes (`faceKey`), and
  the atlas is uploaded at most every 300 ms whatever changed.
- **Images only near you.** Like the live screens: at most 4 desks in view within 9 m fetch their image
  (`transcript.image {id, hash, size: 'thumb'}`), every other one, and a failed fetch, says "Looking at an image" with the
  mascot holding a picture.
- **A label that disagrees with the status is not shown** (only `waiting` while idle or done), so a status event that
  beats the bridge's clearing event never leaves a finished turn's last word on the monitor; an unknown kind is Working.
- **The HUD's waiting count shows only while someone waits**: most of the time it is zero, and a fourth always-on count
  would push the top bar's words out at 1280 px.
- **So do the city badges and the elevator rows** (a teal fourth item, their aria labels and the dedupe key too): a worker
  that waits is in none of working / need you / idle, so without it a floor's badge simply left it out.

## 2026-10-02 — #101: the context meter (what is in the window, and how big the window is)

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is "context in use"? | The **main thread's last request** (Claude: `input + cache_creation + cache_read` of the last main assistant message; Codex: `last_token_usage.total_tokens`), sent raw as `context_tokens`. Not `tokens` (cumulative, cache reads left out) and never rebuilt from the rounded `context_pct`. Subagents are not in it. It is an estimate of what the next turn starts with, not an agent's own figure. |
| 2 | Wire shape | Two additive fields on `Usage` (`context_tokens`, `context_window_estimated`); protocol stays v1, the TS mirror has them optional, an older bridge's payload still draws (share and window, no count). A scalar count and a bool, not pointers: the throttle's `sameUsage` then sees a count-only or flag-only change as a change with no special case. Unknown is 0 / `context_pct` null, and the game hides the meter then: a missing measure is never a 0 %. |
| 3 | How big is a Claude window? | The transcript never says (`message.model` carries no `[1m]`), so it is inferred, in order: `[1m]` on the **main** model; context seen above 200K (proof of the long window, kept across a `/compact` while the model stays); the table; the model family by prefix (`opus`/`sonnet`/`fable` 1M, `haiku` 200K); unknown. Table and family answers are **assumptions** and say so (`context_window_estimated`, `~` on the screen, "Window size assumed" in the tooltip); the others are not. Not consulted: a subagent's `resolvedModel` (it describes the subagent), `settings.json` and the environment (global, and a `/model` or a flag overrides them): each can be stale for the session shown. |
| 4 | The family fallback and prices | Window only. A model the table does not list stays `unknown_model` and is priced as the dearest of its harness: guessing a window must not make a cost look known. A `--pricing` entry with no window is the operator's "unknown" and is not overruled by the family (only by observed context above 200K). `TestUnknownModel` changed accordingly: `claude-opus-9` now has an assumed window and still no price. |
| 5 | `/compact` and `/clear` | The usage parser clears the context at a Claude `compact_boundary` (and a Codex `compacted` / `context_compacted`) and shows nothing until the next measure: the summary's size is unknown (`preTokens` is the old context's) and 0 would be a lie. Totals and cost carry on. `/clear` and `/new` are other agent sessions: the existing session switch already drops the old usage and reads the new transcript afresh. |
| 6 | Codex's baseline | The percentage keeps Codex's own formula (12,000-token baseline, as its TUI and the bridge did), the label shows the raw count and the rollout's own window (258,400 in the sample: never replaced by the model's advertised 1.05M), so `15% · 50k / 258k` is not 50 / 258. The tooltip says the percentage leaves out the baseline; the game knows the harness from the model (a `claude-` prefix is Claude's, as in the pricing table). Showing the adjusted numbers instead would need explicit effective values on the wire; not done. |
| 7 | The look | One slim fill and the number in words, in the transcript strip before the preset and zoom (not a tab stop; `role="meter"`, its text is its value, no live region). Warm at 70 %, hot at 90 % on the **unrounded** share, in the preset's own colours (neutral, sun, red: each 3:1 against the track in Quiet, Terminal and Paper), and the number is always shown, so colour is never the only signal. The window part of the label goes first on a narrow screen (container query), the share last. |
| 8 | Known limits | A Codex worker without an `agent_session` is matched to a rollout by cwd, so two Codex agents in one folder can show each other's meter (no harness-identity redesign here). A Claude session on a 200K plan using a 1M-native model shows `~1M` until its context passes 200K. The desk card and seat HUD still show the old `ctx 4%` text. `gpt-6.1-sol` is not in the pricing table: its window is the rollout's, its price stays unknown until it is verified. |

## 2026-10-02 — #100: the inbox chip (reduced scope)

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Scope | **One small chip, `Inbox 3`** (user, 2 Oct 2026): no list, no dismiss, no desktop notifications, no chime, no hotkey, no bridge change. It is derived in the game from the world the HUD already has (`world/inbox.ts`). |
| 2 | Who is in it | Every worker whose display is `needs_you` (any role: an over-cap ORCH, a freelancer whose report asks) and every **employee** that is `done` (a freelancer's done is its run's business). A sleeping floor's workers and #98's `waiting` ones are not (their display is neither). Order: needs you before done, the longest wait first, wherever you are: N's queue without the "your building first" tier, so the chip means the same thing from every place. |
| 3 | At zero | **Not there** (no `Inbox 0`): the corner stays clean. The number is the plate's red while someone needs you, the plate's text colour when all are done. |
| 4 | Where | Directly under the plan-limits plate, right edges aligned, 6 px below it (the plate's measured height lifts it); in the plate's spot when the plate is switched off or has no reading yet. It goes away wherever the plate steps aside or folds (`ui/corner.ts`, the one rule both follow: a screen dialog, the reading overlay, seated at a laptop, any dialog on a narrow screen), and moves left of the Changes panel with it. It has no edge tab: it comes back when the screen closes. |
| 5 | The click teleports (the exception to N's "no teleporting between floors") | **Straight onto the floor** (`enterFloor`, the doors' ride, no elevator panel), facing the worker, **sat at its laptop** (as E does), from the city, another building, another floor, the lobby or the same floor. N keeps the elevator: it is a walk-along key you use while moving around, where opening the elevator keeps you in the building's geography. The inbox is a "take me to what is waiting" button the user asked to go there and open the screen at once (user, 2 Oct 2026). Since the list (below), it is a row's click that does this, the chip's opens the list. When the laptop cannot be sat at (asleep, no pane, away), you are left facing the worker with the reason. |

## 2026-10-02 — #100: chime and notification settings

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The chime's sound | **Synthesized, no files**: `GameAudio.playUi(sound, volume)`. `needs` is a rising fourth (E5 → A5, triangle, 0.13 s apart), `finished` one quieter sine D5: two tones that say different things without a sample to license or fetch. Each note is an oscillator through its own gain envelope (10 ms attack, exponential decay), into one gain at the chime's volume, into the master: master mute and volume apply, and the radio bus and `stop()` (the receptionist's) never touch it. Nodes are disconnected when the last note ends. |
| 2 | When it is silent | Before the first gesture (no context is made for it: Chrome would start it suspended), while the browser keeps the context locked, muted, disposed, without Web Audio, or at volume 0. Never throws: a chime is not worth an error. Once unlocked, it plays with the window in the background (Chrome keeps a running AudioContext running there). |
| 3 | Settings → Notifications | A "This browser" section after Day and night, bound to `App.inboxPrefs` (`h3d.inbox`). Desktop notifications: the switch, and the browser's permission as the AI section's state tag beside it (Allowed / Not asked / Blocked / Unavailable). **Allow…** shows only while not asked, and calls `Notification.requestPermission()` from the click itself (Chrome asks only inside a gesture); allowed then switches desktop on. Blocked says how to unblock in Chrome (site settings), since the page can't ask again. Unavailable means no Notification API: a page that is not HTTPS or localhost, or a browser without one. The permission is re-read whenever the dialog opens: it changes in the browser's own UI, where the page hears nothing. |
| 4 | The chime's controls | Switch, its own volume (0–100 %, the top bar's slider look, the percentage beside it) and **Test**, which unlocks sound first (the click is a gesture, so a page that never had one can still sound) and plays `needs` at the chime's volume, whether the chime is on or not: you hear it before you choose. A hint says when the master mute will silence it. |
| 5 | Which entries ping | "Needs you" and "Finished" checkboxes for both the notification and the chime; with neither, a hint says they still join the inbox and nothing pings. "Include freelancers when they finish" is off by default (a write-code run's coders and reviewers finish all the time), and is independent of "Finished": it says whether they are listed, not only whether they ping. |
| 6 | A fieldset? | No: a `role="group"` labelled by its heading. `panels.css` styles the floor settings' fieldsets and legends under the same `.settings` class the dialog carries, and they would leak in. |

## 2026-10-02 — #100: pings

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What pings | A **new episode** on a **live** change (`worker.status`, `worker.upsert`, …): a worker into needs you (any role), and an employee into done (a freelancer's done only with "Include freelancers" on). An episode is worker + reason + `since`, so a floor that sleeps and wakes, or a worker that leaves and comes back with the same `status_since`, does not ping again. Settings → Notifications' "needs you" / "finished" switch each kind off; they are read when the ping fires. Pure in `world/inboxPings.ts`, fed the inbox after every change. |
| 2 | Settle delay | A done waits **5 s** before it pings; back to work (or into needs you) meanwhile, and it never does. An agent that ends a turn and starts the next at once is not "finished". A done that leaves the inbox before it pinged with its status kept (#98: waiting on its own background tasks) settles again, once, when it is back: the last task closing is when it is finished. One that already pinged does not ping again (the same episode). A need pings at once. |
| 3 | Snapshots | **Never ping.** The first snapshot and a reconnect's list what is there silently; what is new in them (every entry on the first, the episodes not seen before on a reconnect, a done still settling when the bridge dropped) is **one soft toast**: "While you were away: 3 finished, 1 needs you", only when there is something. It is the documented exception to "toasts only on a live change" (#92's AI chip): the user asked to hear what happened while the game was closed or asleep. A replay (a short drop the bridge can fill from its ring) sends no snapshot; its events are live changes and ping like them, under the burst cap. |
| 4 | Not for you | No ping for the worker you are sat at (also reading its transcript), checked when the ping fires (a done that settles while you sit down is yours). A sleeping floor's workers are never in the inbox. |
| 5 | Burst cap | A burst is 10 s from its first ping. Its first 3 pings are their own notifications; a 4th collapses them into **one** ("4 agents need you" when any does, else "4 agents finished", the names in its body), the first 3 closed, updated in place as more arrive (one tag). **One chime per burst**: its first ping's (needs you or finished). A worker that pings twice in a burst counts once. |
| 6 | Desktop notification | The page's own `new Notification(title, {body, tag: worker id, renotify: false})`: "Chirp needs you" / "Nutmeg finished", body `Building › Floor · reason`. Only with the window not in front (`document.hidden \|\| !document.hasFocus()`); in front the chip pulses and a soft toast says the same. Gated on the setting and `Notification.permission === 'granted'`; the game never asks for the permission by itself (Settings' "Allow…" does). No API, or a constructor that refuses (Android Chrome wants a service worker), shows nothing. Its click focuses the window, closes it and teleports to the worker (`App.goToWorker`); a collapsed burst's opens the inbox's list. Whatever modal dialog is up then (Settings, the elevator, hire, About, a board, …) and the whiteboard are shut first, each through its own close (Settings' fields as on Esc, the whiteboard saved; one it cannot save stays up asking, and the click goes nowhere), so the laptop is on top with the keyboard. |
| 7 | No service worker, no push server | The notifications come from the open page (ticket decision 1: nothing while the game is closed; the inbox has it all on return). A Web Push setup (service worker, VAPID keys, a push service, as depot-manager's PWA has) would add notifications with the game closed and on phones; not needed for a desktop `--app` window that stays open. |

## 2026-10-02 — #100: the inbox list

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The chip's click | **Opens a list** under it (user, 2 Oct 2026: "click on that chip so that it expands and shows me the sessions that need attention, which floor they belong to"), the same navy plate grown, not a dialog. Round 1's walk-along click is gone (N still walks); a **row's** click teleports as round 1's chip did (decision 5 above), now through `App.goToWorker`, which the desktop notification's click uses too. |
| 2 | A row | The laptop's harness sticker (an SVG twin of `render/deskArt.ts`'s: the HUD had none), the name, why as label-maker tape (red for needs you, green for finished), `Building › session` (`Unassigned › session` in the lot: the session is the project name you know, so it is kept there too), and a coarse age (`now`, `4 min`, `2 h`). Why is the first set field of those `workerView` ORs into needs_you: Question (#57), Follow-up (#90), Wrap-up stuck (#83), Report asks; else Needs you (blocked, an over-cap orchestrator). Ages redraw every 20 s while the list is open only. |
| 3 | Conversation, not the terminal | Sitting from the inbox puts the **conversation** (transcript) on screen, in every case, a worker blocked on a screen-only prompt included (user, 3 Oct 2026; it replaces round 1's terminal, whose UX finding was that the remembered reading mode hid it). Only for that sit: what plain E opens next time is not changed (`setMode('transcript', false)`). |
| 4 | Keys and focus | The chip and rows are buttons; the arrows walk them (Down on the chip opens it), Esc shuts it from anywhere but a dialog or a field, and gives the focus back to the chip when it was in the list; a click elsewhere shuts it and leaves the focus where it went. The list keeps its arrows, Enter and Space from the walker. No pointer lock. |
| 5 | Settings | Display → "Show the inbox" (`InboxPrefs.show`, on by default) hides chip and list; "include freelancers" (`InboxPrefs.freelancers`) lists finished freelancers too. |

## 2026-10-02 — #106: dismissing finished rows

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The × | A **Finished** row has a small × (its own button beside the row's: a button cannot hold one), `aria-label` "Dismiss <name>". It hides the row, and the count and chip with it, until that worker's next working → done. A **needs-you row has none**: it leaves when the need is answered. The × is drawn without hover (quiet, the plate's second text colour, brighter with the row and its own hover/focus; 32 px for the thumb), is a stop of the list's arrow-walk (each row, then its ×), and a click on it never goes to the worker. After a dismissal the focus lands on the neighbouring row's × (else its row), not on the page. |
| 2 | Going to it reads it (user, 2 Oct 2026: the inbox is unread mail) | `App.goToWorker` (a row's click, and a desktop notification's) sends `inbox.dismiss` for a worker whose display is `done` when the go-to has succeeded (after the teleport, not when it was refused or the floor went), with the `since` the row had when it was clicked. A needs-you worker is never dismissed by a click. Herdr only turns done → idle when a client views the worker, which this path does not cause, so without this a finished row would stay after you had been to it. |
| 3 | Where it lives | On the **bridge**, SQLite `inbox_dismissed (worker_uuid PK → worker ON DELETE CASCADE, status_since, dismissed_at)`, so it survives a reload, a bridge restart and a second browser. `inbox.dismiss {id, status_since}`; the whole map is the snapshot's `inbox_dismissed`; every client gets `inbox.dismissed {id, status_since}` (docs/protocol.md "Inbox"). Not in `localStorage`: that would be per browser. |
| 4 | The episode | A dismissal is the done episode's `status_since`, not the worker. It holds while the worker is `done` with that very `status_since`; its next working → done has another one and is listed again. The bridge ignores `inbox.dismiss` when the present `status_since` differs or the worker is not done (`{dismissed: false}`, no event), and prunes stale rows at startup, on any status change, and when the worker's workspace closes. The game applies the same match to what it holds (`world/inbox.ts` `isDismissed`), so a late or missing prune never hides a new episode. |
| 5 | Pings | A done row that is dismissed does not ping either (a notification's click dismisses it, and a finish that settles after you have read it is not news). The snapshot's "while you were away" leaves dismissed rows out. |
| 6 | The burst's click over a modal | A collapsed "N agents need you" notification's click shuts every modal and the whiteboard first (`dismissOverlays`, as a single worker's click does through `goToWorker`), then opens the list; nothing happens when the whiteboard stays up asking about its changes. Before, the list opened behind Settings or the elevator. |
| 7 | Known limits | An older bridge (no `inbox.dismiss`) answers `unknown_command`: the × says so in a toast and the row stays; a click's go-to still works. A dismissal is not undone from the game. |

## 2026-10-02 — #84: worker names never carry a number

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Out of names | **Recycle, never suffix.** When every name of the list is held, `world.PickName` takes the held name **released longest ago** (hold ended early; ties go to the earlier list entry). Its old owner's claim is overwritten (same path as "hold ran out and a newcomer took it"), so the old owner gets a new name if it ever returns. |
| 2 | Every name owned by a live worker (more than 100 on one floor) | A name of a live worker is **never** recycled (names stay unique on a floor). The newcomer gets a **two-word name** of two list entries (`"Pixel Mochi"`), the first one not held from its start position. Chosen over an error: a hire or a returning worker must always get a name, and 100×99 compounds are far beyond the floor hard cap. `IsName` accepts them. |
| 3 | Deliberate departures | A worker sent home (`worker.fire`, a wrap-up's send home: the `removed` event with reason `sent_home`) is released **as already held for the whole hold** (`released = now − name hold`), so its name is free at once and the first to be recycled. Crash, pane close, restart, sleep keep the full hold. |
| 4 | Existing `X 2` / `X 3` names | Kept for the life of their worker; `IsName` still accepts the suffix, nothing hands one out. Not renamed. |
| 5 | Not done | No new names (issue option 3) and the default name hold stays 7 days (option 4). |

## 2026-10-02 — #29 (M6c): freelancers walk their report to the orchestrator's desk

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | An orchestrator on another floor, in another building, or nobody watching | **No walk.** The folder lands on the tray at once (like `Arrivals.skip`); a freelancer whose orchestrator is not on its floor keeps today's seated hold-out. Same for the city view, a sleeping floor, or a floor whose interior is not loaded (`DeliveryBook.resolve`). |
| 2 | Does the orchestrator stand up to take the folder | **Never.** It stays seated: busy (`working`) it swivels in its chair a quarter turn to the freelancer, reaches, takes the folder and drops it on the tray as the chair swings back. Blocked, waiting for you, asleep, idle or done it does not react: the freelancer puts the folder on the tray itself, a little sooner. |
| 3 | A report with `input_required` | **Does not walk.** Today's `blocked` wins: the freelancer stays at its desk with its hand up. If the question is answered later (same `report.at`), the report plays then. |
| 4 | Trigger | `pending → ready`, or a new `report.at`, on a live `worker.upsert`, once per worker id + `report.at` (`world/delivery.ts`). A report already `ready` in a snapshot (the first, or a reconnect's) plays nothing: the freelancer sits empty-handed, the folder is on the tray. A reconnect snapshot also ends the walks in flight. A report is **remembered as played** (`DeliveryBook.played`, apart from the tray) for as long as its freelancer is in the world: a snapshot that has the report pending, then ready again with the same `at`, puts the folder back on the tray and plays nothing; a genuinely new `at` plays. The memory is dropped when the worker is gone (snapshot or removal). |
| 5 | Parallel reviewers | **One walk at a time per floor, whole round.** The second (of the same orchestrator or of another one on that floor) waits at its own desk with the folder held out towards the orchestrator (the old hold-out pose) until the first has sat down, then does its own round. This is a change from the issue's "waits a step back": a second walker on the same hand-over spot would meet the first one coming back in a narrow aisle, so they never share the way. Reports to different orchestrators on a floor queue the same way: routes of two walks would meet in the aisles, and the walkers must never overlap, so there is no route reservation, just the one-walk rule (oldest report first; another floor's walk is independent). |
| 6 | The hand-over spot | Beside the orchestrator's chair on its desk's **dock side** (the pod's outer side: the aisle), level with the chair, 0.8 m from it (`HAND_LATERAL`), facing it. Clear of the chair and the desk; the chair swivels a quarter turn to it. |
| 7 | The route | `deskToDeskRoute` (`world/route.ts`, pure): out of the pod into the aisle behind the chair as `walkOutRoute` does, then A\* on a 20 cm grid that keeps the walker's width plus 12 cm off every desk, prop, post and other seat, pulled tight into straight segments, then in to the spot. The way back is `reverseRoute`. `walkOutRoute(…, from)` takes a start point (mid-delivery; from up in the corner office it goes back down the stair first). |
| 8 | Timing | Walk at 1.9 m/s (`DELIVER_SPEED`; 2–3 s each way on a pod), stand 0.6 s (chair pushed back), hand-over 1.1 s, sit 0.5 s: about 7 s a round on a pod. |
| 9 | A walk-out during a delivery | A hand-over under way is finished (the walk-out is chained and starts from the spot). At any other step the delivery ends where the freelancer stands and the walk-out starts there (`standing`: walk and fade, no packing or wave). Either way the folder lands on the tray, and stays there until the freelancer has left (`DeliveryBook.close` / `settle`); a folder already on the tray goes at once with its freelancer's workspace, except the one of a delivery walk still going (the hand-over finishing, the walk back): that stays too, even when it landed already. The chained walk-out survives whatever the book still has of its report (`reconcileDeliveries` never cancels a delivery whose freelancer is leaving). |
| 10 | The in-tray | On every orchestrator desk, where the other workers keep a notebook (an orchestrator has none now). One folder per report handed in, **the verdict colour on its front edge**, the last 5 shown (`TRAY_CAP`), a newer report of the same freelancer replaces its folder. Instanced (`render/tray.ts`: tray, folders, edges); the walker's folder flying to it is two more instanced parts of the arrivals. The walk reuses `Arrivals` and the instanced crowd (kind `deliver`); the desk stays the world's (laptop live, card, pick) and only the figure and the chair move (`OfficeView.setOut`). |
| 11 | **The corner seat (game-only)** | In the game as it stands only **you** sit in the corner office. The issue wants an orchestrator "in the corner office", so: a worker with the optional field `corner: true` (`Worker.corner`, never sent by the bridge; fixtures, the simulator and tests) whose role is `orch` sits at the mezzanine's orchestrator desk (`layout.mezzanine.orch`) instead of at its desk slot. Everything index-based keeps its desk index (card, pick, laptop-less monitor); only where it is drawn changes (`seatOf`). Its floor desk is gone, its tray is up there, no live screen is drawn for it, E at its laptop says it sits in the corner office, and reports reach it by the mezzanine stair and the glass door (which opens for the walker as it does for you). Your own desk is **not** a destination. `sim.crew` `{step: "hire", corner: true}` makes the simulator's own corner orchestrator; `simcrew=<ms>` uses it every other round. |
| 12 | Not done | A paper plane to another floor, a walk through the elevator, the orchestrator standing up, a queue spot "a step back" (see 5). |
| 13 | The world changes under a walk | One path (`App.reconcileDeliveries`, after every worker or floor event and after an office rebuild) resolves **at once** any delivery that no longer fits: its freelancer or its orchestrator moved desk (or floor), the orchestrator is gone, the corner flag changed, the floor went to sleep, you are not looking at the floor any more, or **the office was rebuilt for a new layout** (a hire or a leave that re-flows the pods: the route, hand-over spot and tray coordinates were for the old one). Resolving = the walk ends where it is, the folder is on the tray, the freelancer is seated where the world seats it now, the orchestrator faces its desk; no hand-over at a stale spot, no teleport at the end. The queue follows (waiting reports start, or resolve too when nobody can see them). A freelancer whose workspace closed keeps its chained walk-out. A delivery that yielded its desk (`yieldDesk`) draws no more and gives the chair back; only someone **else** at its desk yields it: its own freelancer's upsert (a report update, a question appearing) at the same desk never does. |

## 2026-10-02 — #110: pack up on request

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Shape | `internal/packup` is `internal/followup`'s: `Run` (the classifier gate, then the LLM), `Service` (the settings row, the env override, readiness, the 30-minute test of the models in use, `ai.ErrNotReady` before any call when off or not ready), and it takes only `ai`'s interfaces (`TestPackupUsesOnlyAIInterfaces`). The hub's `packup.*` commands mirror `followups.*`; `packup.update` is its own event type (`proto.PackupUpdate`, as `activity.update` is), since its flat fields are `followups.update`'s. |
| 2 | The LLM's answer | **Spans to remove, never a rewrite.** Each must be an exact substring of what the models saw, found in order after the previous one; image stand-ins may not be cut. The bridge cuts them and tidies only whitespace and dangling separators at the joins. Anything invalid, any failure and the time bound (#12) are no match: the prompt goes as written. A failure never eats a prompt. |
| 3 | Offsets | `removed` is in UTF-16 code units, the game's string indexes, so the confirm strikes the right characters without re-encoding. The game still checks the ranges (sorted, apart, inside the text) and sends as written when they are not. |
| 4 | Images | The models see `[Image #n]` for each marker, never its path; a span over one is invalid. The marker stays in the task as it was. |
| 5 | Nothing left | `matched: true` with no task: the game opens the normal send-home dialog, nothing is sent. |
| 6 | Who | The wrap-up's workers: Claude or Codex agents, freelancers skipped (`world.Freelancer`), checked on the bridge and, to spare the round trip, in the game. |
| 7 | The hand-off | "Send and go home" is `worker.wrapup` with the task, a blank line, and the cleanup request ("Once that is done: " + the wrap-up's own prompt). No new teardown, no turn-end tracking. The #83 footer now reads "or, if the task did not complete, anything could not be stopped or needs a decision", so a failed task on a clean, pushed tree reports BLOCKED and the worker stays. A wrap-up armed by an older bridge with the old footer and resumed after the upgrade is no longer recognised and times out to needs you. |
| 8 | Threshold | **0.85 by default**, higher than follow-ups' 0.7: a false positive costs one dismiss, a false negative is today's behaviour. The gate question was calibrated against `jev-latest` and `jev-preview` in 1.20.1 (the first wording scored the ticket's "leave the office and clean up the worktree" at 0.3; the reworded one 0.93, 30 of 31 samples right at 0.75, skips at most 0.2); the threshold itself is untested beyond that: the labelled samples (31 prompts) are in `packup.Samples`, and `packup.test`'s `right` of `total` at a threshold is the calibration to run live. |
| 9 | The test | Only the four core samples gate a pass (each match cut to its exact task, the negative under the threshold); the rest are reported, so a model that misreads one paraphrase can still be switched on, and the count shows how good the threshold is. The samples are classified four at a time; only the three core matches go to the LLM. |
| 10 | Seated | The confirm (and the send home for a prompt with nothing left) keeps you at the laptop you sent from: any other dialog stands you up, which would drop the prompt box and its draft. The dialog still owns the keys. |
| 11 | Where it applies | The laptop's prompt box and the P overlay (both use the shared composer). A hire's first prompt is later (its worktree exists only after the hire); prompts typed into the terminal are already sent. |
| 12 | The 1.5 s bound | **The game holds a prompt at most 1.5 s from Send** (`packup.detect`'s timeout in `net/client.ts`): past it the prompt goes as written and a later ack, a match included, is dropped, so no confirm opens. The bridge bounds its models at **1.2 s** (`packup.DetectTimeout`), leaving its no match 0.3 s to reach the game; the model bound alone could not cover the hub's queue or a slow link. |
| 13 | Logs | The bridge logs a detect's score, whether it matched and the task's length; a failure only as a fixed code (`ai.Kind`, or `llm_<kind>`, `bad_answer`, `bad_part` for an extraction): never an error's text, since a provider may quote the prompt in it. |

## 2026-10-03 — #116: a new hire takes you to its transcript

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The setting | Settings → Display → "Go to a new hire after hiring" (`HirePrefs.goToNew`, `ui/hirePrefs.ts`, key `h3d.hire.goto`, browser-stored like the other display prefs). **On by default**; only "off" is stored. Off is today's behaviour exactly: the dialog closes, the toast, you stay where you are. |
| 2 | When it fires | After `worker.hire` is acknowledged **and the hire dialog is still the one that sent it** (`still` in the ack handler, `ui/hire.ts`): `HireHost.goTo(ack.id)`, wired in `app.ts` to `goToNewHire`, which ends in `goToWorker(id, 'transcript')` (the inbox's go-to: faces the worker, sits at its laptop, its conversation on screen). Also for `prompted: false` (the error toast stays; you land where you can prompt it with P). A hire with a first task lands on the transcript with that task as its first prompt, one without on an empty transcript. |
| 3 | Not on late acks | If you pressed Esc, moved on or opened another dialog (`still` false), the ack only toasts, as before: a late ack never yanks you. The same while it waits (4): the moment you act it is gone **for good**, not just while the state looks wrong at a poll: a key, a press or the wheel (`onPlayerAct`, `ui/hireGoto.ts`), any dialog taking `open` (so a Settings or About that opened and closed again between two polls counts), or a walker pose that is no longer the one you had when the ack came (moved, turned, sat). Besides, at every look and at fire time, a dialog of the game, **any open modal `<dialog>`** (Settings and About are not in `dialogOpen`, yet `dismissOverlays` would close them and seat you), the elevator or another panel, a seat you took, an elevator ride or leaving the floor drops it. Nothing is queued. |
| 4 | Waits for the upsert and the arrival | `goToNewHire` polls every 100 ms for at most **6 s** (`HIRE_GOTO_WAIT_MS`, `ui/hireGoto.ts`) until the worker is in the store (`worker.upsert`) **and** its drop-in has finished (`!arrivals.has(id)`, desk not in `awayDesks()`), then calls `goToWorker`; on timeout the toast stands alone. The deadline and your acting are checked before readiness at every look (`waitForHire`, `ui/hireGoto.ts`), so a timer that runs late (throttled or resumed tab) never acts on a readiness that came after the 6 s. *Ack before upsert?* On the bridge the ack follows the upsert (`runHire` waits for `op.seen`, set in the same pass that builds the upsert, and the stream is ordered), but the protocol does not promise it and the dialog already copes with a missing `workerView` (`w` null: the name from the form), so the game does not assume it. *`sitAt` during the arrival?* It does not fail for the drop-in itself (`whyNot` only refuses a missing worker, an `awayDesks()` desk, a corner desk, no pane, a sleeping floor; probed on the simulator: it sat 0.2 s into the drop), but the seat's camera would land on the chair while the figure still falls into it, so the go-to waits for the end of the arrival. Only a worker not yet in the store, or its desk still held `away` (the hire's own reservation, before the world shows it), makes `sitAt` toast "is not at the desk right now". |
| 5 | A go-to that cannot sit | `goToWorker` returning false, or leaving you facing the worker (asleep floor, no pane yet), is not an error: nothing is toasted by the hook and the toast already says "Here they come." (`sitAt`'s own reason shows as for any go-to). |
| 6 | Not for orchestrated runs | The start-run dialog does its own `followOrch` on start (you are turned to face its desk); it has no `goTo`, so there is no second jump. Its "hire at desk" tab opens the hire dialog, which does go. |
| 7 | Where it is not | Hiring for another floor from where you stand is not supported (`openHire` uses `hereSession()`), so the new worker is always on your floor; N, the inbox and E are unchanged. The #54 rule (a hire never moves you off your spot) still holds with the setting off, and with it on the move is the explicit go-to. |

## 2026-10-03 — #118 (phase 1): the floor overview

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Where the screen hangs | The **west wall of the north-west lounge**, over the coffee counter and the radio, beside the teal sofa: every office floor has it (a prop of `floorLayout`, id `screen.overview`; not `board.*`, which would make it a board target). |
| 2 | How big | As big as the blank stretch allows, from one pure function of the outline's depth (`overviewScreenBox`, `world/layout.ts`, using `openingsFor`): along the wall from 0.4 m off the north-west corner to 0.4 m short of the frame (sill and jambs, 5 cm past the hole) of the northernmost west window; 1.6 m up (over the coffee machine, the mugs and the radio's antenna, 1.4 m) to 5.6 m (under the window heads at 5.95, well under the 6.5 m ceiling and its light strips). No fixed aspect: 4.75 × 4 m at depth 17.8 (the worst), 7.35 × 4 m at 14; the drawing adapts. A bezel box 8 cm off the wall, facing +x. Tested over depths 14 / 17.8 / 23.1 / 28.4; the radio stays the nearest pick, the mezzanine's posts keep clear (every prop is in their avoid list). |
| 3 | What the wall shows | Canvas textures of the same tree as the page (`render/overviewScreen.ts`): a header (the floor's name, the counts) and the board, a line per worker with its light, name, role chip, ticket, task / now line and `since`. Unlit `MeshBasicMaterial`, `toneMapped: false`, its own program cache key (the r186 workaround of `render/tv.ts`); it adds no light (M17). Drawn in the HUD's face (the page's Archivo and Departure Mono load only when the page opens). **Canvas work only on a change of what it shows**, at most once a second, the since column on the wall's own minute (a floor of 60 redraws once a minute, not once per row), never for the live activity chip (the page's): nothing is drawn or uploaded to blink or to scroll. The board is one tall canvas of the rows seen through a window (the texture's repeat and offset): rows go one line each when two do not fit (the ticket's key, then the task / now line: what a worker does now is never dropped for the ticket's title), and the window **scrolls** slowly down and jumps back to the top when even those do not (4 s rests), so no row is cut off unsaid. A floor with more rows than one canvas holds (4096 px, about 120 one-line rows on the 4 m wall) is drawn in **pages** of that many, each starting a window before the last one ended: the window scrolls down a page, the next is drawn there (a redraw per page turn), and after the last it goes back to the first, so every row comes by in a cycle. An orchestrator's chip carries its round (`ORCH r2`), and its two-line meta has phase, round, pass and over cap. The working lights blink by showing and hiding a cover layer (dark discs over those lights only) at the page's rhythm (1.1 s). Battery saver or reduced motion: steady lights, the window jumps instead of gliding. While it is out of view or spans under 30 % of the view's height it neither redraws nor scrolls (a busy floor's desk monitors stay the only motion, `m98-monitor`).
