Not in the world snapshot: the list matters only while the popup is open, and it is asked once per popup |
| 5 | What does a pick put in? | **Plain text, not a file chip**: the folder when the repo is cloned there (or for a folder-only floor), else `https://github.com/owner/repo`, and a space. Chips are checkout-relative `@path` mentions (#138's translator strips the `@` for Codex only inside the worker's checkout); an absolute path from another floor would reach Codex as `@/abs/…`. A path with whitespace goes in double quotes; one with `"` or a line break is not offered |
| 8 | Other buildings | A **shared** switch, "Repositories from other buildings", off by default (and when never set), stored on the bridge (`repo_refs_settings`, one row) like #155's Codex server switch: `repo_refs` in the snapshot, `repo_refs.get` / `.set {all_buildings}`, `repo_refs.update` to every client, an audit row per call, a Settings section marked Bridge. An open `@r:` popup follows a change |

## 2026-10-12 — #40: user-created task levels, and Settings going blank after a click

Corrects #248 rows 1–2: "Very trivial", "Trivial" and "Slightly complex" were examples of levels the player wanted to
add, never three mandatory slots.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What a level is | `{ id, name, harness, model, effort }` in **an ordered list that is the player's** (`world/taskLevels.ts`, `ui/taskLevels.ts`). The id is generated, stable and opaque (`crypto.randomUUID()`, else a time-and-random fallback), never shown and never derived from the name; `TaskLevelId` is a `string`. A name is trimmed, not empty, at most 40 characters and unique ignoring case (`nameProblem`). A level can be **not set yet** (named, its combination empty): it is stored and shown in Settings as "Not set", never offered by Hire / Resolve. At most 20 levels. A fresh browser has **none**: the examples are never created. |
| 2 | Storage and migration | Same key `h3d.hire.taskLevels`, now `{ "v": 2, "levels": [{id, name, harness, model, effort}, …], "default": id \| null }`, a level not set yet with three empty strings; no levels leaves no key. Read guarded as before: missing, throwing or malformed storage, or another version, is no levels; a malformed entry (no id, a duplicate id, a name that fails the rules or repeats one, a field that is not a string) is dropped and the good ones kept, a half-set combination reads as not set, a `default` naming no level reads as none. **#248's value** (an object keyed `very_trivial` / `trivial` / `slightly_complex`, no `v`) migrates when read: each *set* slot becomes a level named "Very trivial" / "Trivial" / "Slightly complex", in that order, unset slots are dropped, `default` is the migrated Trivial when it was set (else none), and the new shape is stored (guarded). |
| 3 | The default, the requested level and the fallback | Exactly one level can be marked **Default**; none marked: the **first in the list** is the default. Settings shows the effective default checked; clicking it (or Space) marks it, so it stays the default when the rows move. Resolve conflicts asks for the default: `HirePrefill.level` is `'default' \| <id>` (an id no longer in the list also means the default). A requested level that cannot be used falls back to the **nearest usable one by list position, the earlier before the later at each distance** (`levelOrder(ids, requested)` / `preselectLevel`); a level not set yet counts as a place, never as a pick. None usable: the usual pickers with the note and "Open Task levels"; no level set: the usual pickers and no "Choose a more complex model…" (both unchanged). The Hire dialog's chips are the set levels in list order, each "Name — Model · effort". |
| 4 | Settings → Task levels | Per row: the **name** as a field in place (saved on Enter, on leaving the field and on change; a name that fails the rules shows why under it, `aria-invalid`, and is not saved; Esc with an edit in progress goes back to the saved name and keeps the dialog open, a second Esc closes it), its state badge, **Default** (one native radio group across the rows: the arrow keys move the mark), **Move up / Move down**, **Clear** (unsets the combination, keeps the level and its name) and **Remove** (at once; the focus goes to the next row's name, else the previous one's, else Add level), then the Hire dialog's Harness chips, Model field and Effort chips as before. **Add level** appends a level not set yet named "Level N" (the first free number) and puts the keyboard on its name, selected. The buttons' names carry the level's name ("Move Quick fix up"). The ends and the cap are `aria-disabled`, not `disabled`, so a button that reaches an end keeps the focus (the board statuses editor's rule); at the cap a hint says why. Rows live as long as their level and are updated in place (only the fields are redrawn, when they change), so a redraw never replaces the control a click is on its way to, nor a name being typed. |
| 5 | The blank dialog: cause | A chip (`chips` in `ui/hire.ts`) is a `.dd-chip` label around a `.visually-hidden` radio, which is absolutely positioned (`panels.css`). The label set no containing block, so the radio's was the dialog: it sat outside the scrolling `.settings-body`, at its unscrolled place far below the window. Focusing it (a label click, an arrow key, the redraw's focus restore) scrolled the nearest scroll container of *its* containing-block chain, the outer dialog, which is `overflow: hidden` (still scrollable by script and focus): header and body went up out of the window. Probes: outer `scrollTop` 1122 after one harness chip click. `focus({ preventScroll: true })` on the redraw alone could not help: native label-click focus has no such option. |
| 6 | The blank dialog: fix | Two independent fixes, each enough on its own in the probes, both kept: **`.dd-chip { position: relative }`** (`hire.css`, shared with the Hire dialog: the radio stays inside the chip's own scroller), and **`dialog.settings { overflow: clip }`** (`settings.css`, scoped to Settings: it clips like `hidden` but is no scroll container, so nothing can scroll the window itself; probed, the scroll does not move to the page instead). The clip also stops two other outer scrolls seen while testing: opening Settings in the Ubuntu theme (a few pixels) and `scrollIntoView` of a section. Every focus move of the section uses `focus({ preventScroll: true })` and then scrolls only the nearest scrolling ancestor itself (rect deltas, never `scrollIntoView`); a focused control whose row moves under it is brought back into view the same way. The Hire dialog did not reproduce the blank window in probes (unfixed, 390 × 640); the chip rule covers it anyway. Regression: `e2e/h40-task-levels.spec.ts` measures the dialog's `scrollTop`, header and body tops right after each mouse click (box read, then `page.mouse.click`, no locator action between) and each key, for every chip kind and management control, in Quiet and Ubuntu, at 1280 × 800 and 390 × 640; with both CSS rules taken out its Settings tests fail. |

## 2026-10-14 — #39: completion in the Hire, Start run and Prompt dialogs

The Hire worker First task, Start run's Extra instructions and editable First prompt, and the Prompt (P) overlay
complete `/` skills, `@` files and `@r:` repositories as the transcript does, and what is picked before a hire reaches
the worker in its first prompt. Supersedes #138 row 7 for first prompts and #247's "transcript only".

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Lookups with no worker | **New session-keyed ops** `hire.commands {session, harness, cwd?, worktree?}`, `hire.files {session, cwd?, worktree?, query, limit?}`, `hire.repos {session}`, not optional args on `worker.*`: the worker ops stay keyed by a worker, the new ones by what a hire would get. They reuse the same catalog, file index and repo scope (`Options.Commands`, `Options.Files`, `repoCandidates`), validated as `worker.hire` (`hireCwd`, the harness catalog, `herdr.CanStart`) but **without** `hireFloor`'s busy gate (a lookup changes nothing and must answer while another hire runs). `hire.commands` and `hire.files` want a running floor (as their `worker.*` twins); `hire.repos` does not (stored settings only, as `worker.repos`). `worktree` was added to the brief's arg sets so the bridge, not the client, resolves the lookup folder |
| 2 | Which folder for a worktree hire | Its **repository root** (`Git.TopLevel(cwd)`, as `runHire`): the worktree does not exist before the hire. Its files are the main checkout's, so an uncommitted file there is offered but is not in the new worktree, and its `@path` reaches Codex as typed |
| 3 | When is skill discovery asked | **Lazily**, when a `/` popup opens (never on dialog open or `hire.prepare`): optional assistance must not cost a hire anything. A cached list shows at once and refreshes behind it; the first one says "Loading skills…"; a failure says why and offers "Try again", in the popup only |
| 4 | First-prompt translation | **Once, when the hire sends it**, in `runHire` against the final checkout (after `CreateWorktree`), only what a person typed: `worker.hire`'s prompt, `run.start`'s `extra` inside the composed prompt (`hireOp.compose`), or an edited `run.start` prompt whole. The ticket the bridge composes (issue URL, a Kaneo task's text) is never translated. Skills come from a new cache-only path (`Catalog.Cached`, `Prompts.TranslateCached`): the list held for the lookup context however old, never a probe, so a hire never waits; with none cached `/skill` goes as typed (fail-open). The write-code call follows the harness table's `SkillCall` instead of a `"codex"` literal |
| 5 | A stalled Codex first prompt | `pressUntilWorking` presses the harness's **retry keys** (`esc enter` after a `$skill`, through `Options.Term`), as `worker.prompt`'s retry, else Enter |
| 6 | Stale results | Each dialog's completion has a **context** (floor, harness, and the folder and worktree choice `worker.hire` would carry: the Folder as typed, never only the last one `hire.prepare` checked; the P overlay's worker) read live. A change starts a new generation: an open popup asks again, a reply of the old one is dropped when it comes, and a row of the old one puts nothing in (every row carries its generation; checked at insert time, not only when drawn). No wire cancellation: superseded replies are ignored |
| 7 | One command catalog | The app's `CommandCatalogs` is shared by the transcript, the P overlay (the worker's list) and the hire dialogs (lists keyed by context), so a worker's list is asked once whichever opens first |
| 8 | `/` vs `@` | `/` is the harness's catalog only (its global skills too), `@` the context folder's files only: a skill's own file outside the folder is never offered as a file. The footers say so ("Skills and commands · type @ for files", "Files in this checkout · type r: …") |
| 9 | Popup in a dialog | The rows' styles moved from `transcript.css` to `laptop/completionRows.css` (imported by the sources), and a dialog's popup is bounded by its scrolling form, so it opens above the caret when below would be clipped |

## 2026-10-14 — #9 (part 3 of #3): handoffs written by the configured LLM

"Use configured LLM" in the F dialog: the AI integration's LLM writes a handoff from the worker's reduced, scrubbed
transcript, without prompting the worker, filed in #223's cabinet. The feature ships **off**; whether it is good
enough to recommend is the paid spike's call (`docs/research/handoff-summarizer.md`, still pending).

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Where the code lives | A new package `internal/handoffllm`, not "behind `internal/handoff`" as the ticket says: `internal/handoff` must stay a leaf (`TestHandoffIsALeaf`: the standard library and the store), and the reducer reads transcripts while the feature talks to `ai.LLM`. The package has two halves with their own import rules: the reducer (`reduce*.go`, `scrub*.go`, `budget*.go`, `doc.go`; the standard library, `internal/jsonl`, `internal/transcript`; `TestReducerIsALeaf`, per file) and the feature (`generate.go`, `prompt.go`, `validate.go`; `internal/ai`'s interfaces only, as the other features). `internal/handoff` is still the one writer of the cabinet. `proto` cannot import the package (`handoffllm` → `transcript` → `proto`), so it mirrors the view, the test result and the codes, and `TestHandoffLLMProtocolMirrorsTheFeature` keeps them equal. |
| 2 | Reduction policy | `internal/handoffllm/doc.go` is the policy and the text layout. Two points differ from the ticket's wording: a Codex rollout's readable rows **before** a compaction stay (the `compacted` item's `message` is often empty; `replacement_history` adds only messages not seen), and a Claude snapshot seeded from a compaction summary keeps the earlier genuine prompts as unpinned "before compaction" messages, the first to go under budget pressure (never a reason to refuse). Every genuine prompt after the summary and the final reply are pinned whole: a snapshot that cannot fit them is refused (`too_large`, the dialog says to raise the budget or save the last message), never cut. The bridge checks the candidate is still the transcript's latest reply (`transcript.StillLatest`) before every reduction; the reducer checks the file and the snapshot's end. |
| 3 | The gate | Copied from the summaries (#118): its own switch (`handoff_llm_settings`), off by default; switching on needs the LLM ready and a passing `handoff_llm.test` within 30 minutes of the config in use: the LLM's fingerprint, the effort and the completion budget (the transcript budget is not part of it: the test sends a synthetic sample). Unlike the summaries, the switch is **bound** to that config (its key is stored, a hash): another key, model, endpoint, effort or budget makes it `untested`, and nothing is sent until a test passes again (which binds it anew). `HERDR_WORLD_HANDOFF_LLM_ENABLED` owns the switch headless (no test; the LLM still has to be ready). Readiness is checked when asked, when the call goes (`Service.Generate`) and before filing. |
| 4 | Timeout and retry | Two per-request fields of `ai.Request`, used by this feature only: `Timeout` (90 s here, `handoffllm.Timeout`, **provisional** until the spike measures latencies; every other call keeps the shared 15 s) and `NoUncertainRetry`: no second attempt after an outcome the provider may have processed and billed (a timeout, a dropped connection, a 5xx); a refused connection or an unresolved name (`neverSent`) and the response_format fallback may still retry. Plus `RedactKey` (the role's own key replaced in the messages: the feature cannot see it) and `NoPartial` (a cut-off answer is `truncated`, never filed short) and `BudgetWins` (the setting, not an `extra_body`'s budget). |
| 5 | Scheduling | One LLM handoff at a time over the bridge (the feature's `busy`, and the hub's `running` job): a second is `busy`, and the preview says so. A 90 s call then holds at most one of the AI integration's two shared slots, so the follow-up questions and pack up keep one. No queue: a queued paid call the person cannot see is worse than a clear "one at a time". |
| 6 | Jobs and idempotency | A table of its own, `handoff_llm_job`, not a generalised `handoff_request`: that one's `path NOT NULL UNIQUE`, `nonce` and `state CHECK ('pending','filed','failed')` are the agent's, and SQLite cannot alter a CHECK. One row per op, stored before the call, its audit row begun first and nothing that can fail between the row and the job's start (a stored `running` job always runs): if either insert fails the command fails with nothing stored and nothing sent, and the op may be used again. The op answers a repeated submit with its job (`existing`) and never calls twice; the same op with another worker or other arguments (a SHA-256 of them: the instruction is never stored) is `bad_request`. States `queued`/`running`/`filed`/`failed`/`cancelled`, a code and a short message (the provider's own words dropped: only the part before its first colon, and never the model's text). A restart fails every unfinished job (`handoff_llm_interrupted`): never resent. Settled rows are kept 30 days; a worker shows its latest job (`Worker.llm_handoff`) for a day after a restart. "Try again" in the dialog is a new op, and says it sends a new request. |
| 7 | Stale answers | The job keeps the LLM's fingerprint and the worker's agent session it started with. An AI change (`trackAI`), another agent session (the worker's upsert) or the worker leaving cancels it; and before filing the hub checks again (the job not stopped, the worker there under that session, the fingerprint the same, the feature ready): a late answer is never filed under another session or config (`handoff_llm_stale`). Once those checks pass the job is `filing` and no hook cancels it. A new turn of the same worker does not void it: the snapshot is fixed at the candidate, and the provenance says so. |
| 8 | Prompt and output | `PromptVersion` `h3d-handoff-llm-1`: the ticket's prompt plus the format: one `# ` title of at most 80 characters and the H2 sections `Goal and current state`, `Decisions`, `Verification`, `Open questions`, `Next steps`, `Suggested skills` (`"None."` rather than a missing one). The facts, the transcript and the focus are separate blocks between `<<<NAME-<nonce>` / `NAME-<nonce>>>>`, the nonce drawn per request (crypto/rand) and absent from the data; the reducer's text has no control characters and defuses lines starting `===`. The facts say the git state was captured now (`git status`, the branch) apart from the branch herdr reported, the artifacts as verified (a result shows success: an exit 0, a pass line, an edit's success line, a commit or URL it made) or unverified (only the call, a run still running in the background, an empty result or one that shows no outcome: the absence of a failure is never a success), the coverage gaps; the cached skill list only when the command catalog has one (never a probe). `Validate` proves the shape, not the facts: one whole-answer code fence is taken off, nothing else is changed. |
| 9 | Filing and provenance | `handoff.Service.File` with `source: llm`, the worker's metadata, the candidate as `source_turn`, the document's hash as `source_hash` (so a second LLM handoff of the same reply with another focus is not taken for the first), and the new front-matter keys `llm_model`, `llm_host` (the host only), `prompt_version`, `snapshot`, `coverage`, `redactions` (`handoff.ProvenanceKeys`, read back by `handoff.get` as `llm`). No new column: the cabinet's detail reads them from the file. The call is made before `File`, never under its lock. The send home only after the file, row and index, through `handoffSendHome`. |
| 10 | Audit | `worker.handoff`'s args keep the instruction's length only, in every mode (the agent mode logged it verbatim before). An LLM job's row: model, host, prompt version, payload/sent/answer/document bytes, elapsed time, the outcome code, the redaction counts, the coverage numbers, the call's sanitized diagnostics. Never the transcript, the instruction, the document or a key (`TestLLMHandoffFilesWithProvenance`, `TestLLMHandoffFailures` and the real-reducer test grep the logs, the audit log, the wire and the job rows). |
| 11 | Dialog | "Use configured LLM" is selectable only while the feature is `ready`; otherwise it says exactly why (off, LLM not set up, untested, no such bridge) with a button to Settings. It is never chosen for you; literal stays the default. The preview names the recipient (model at host), the reduced size and estimated tokens against the budget, what was kept and left out (ranges and why), the gaps, what the scrub removed, and what is sent (a disclosure). A failure keeps the dialog with the reason; reopening shows a running job, or one that settled in the last 10 minutes. |
| 12 | Settings | "LLM handoffs", after the floor overview summaries: the switch, what it needs, what is sent and to whom above it (the categories, what is left out, and that the scrub is pattern-based and can miss a secret), the transcript budget in KB, the completion budget, the effort, Test. |
| 13 | Spike and rollout | `cmd/handoff-spike` reduces given JSONL files and prints numbers only; `--send` (with an endpoint, model and key file) calls the LLM through the feature itself. No paid call was made for this change: acceptance criterion 1 of #9 (quality, latency and cost across ~5 sessions, and a go/no-go) stays open, and the feature ships off by default until it is decided. |

## 2026-10-14 — #21: Codex code changes in the transcript

Codex's direct `apply_patch` showed its input as a diff (no line numbers, shown as applied whatever happened), and
the patches a code-mode `exec` script runs (`tools.apply_patch(...)`, the only form since codex-cli 0.152) showed
nothing. Now what a patch did comes from what Codex recorded, through #171's shared view (no second renderer, no new
preference). Evidence: a read-only survey of 3,771 local rollouts (codex-cli 0.145–0.162.1) and Codex's source at
`rust-v0.160.1`, `0.161.0`, `0.162.1`. Code: `internal/transcript/codexpatch.go` (`fileChangeDiffs`, `unifiedHunks`,
`parsePatch`, `patchLiterals`, `jsString`), `codex.go` (`codexPatchEvent`, the `exec` call and output rules), `log.go`
(`linkScript`), `internal/proto/transcript.go` (`DiffRename`, `TranscriptDiff.To`, `TranscriptChanges.Computed`),
`game/src/laptop/transcriptRender.ts` (`changeFile`, `changesBlock`, `emptyNote`); tests
`internal/transcript/codexpatch_test.go`, `codexchanges_test.go` (fixtures `testdata/codex/patch_*.jsonl`),
`laptop/transcriptChanges.test.ts`, `tools/fixture-transcript.test.ts`, `e2e/h21-codex-changes.spec.ts` (fixture
timeline `demo-h21`: `?ws=%2Ffixture%2Fdemo-h171%3Fsim%3D1%26simtx%3Ddemo-h21`, sit at Nibble on herdr-3d).

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The source of a change | **What Codex recorded**: `event_msg` `item_completed` with a `FileChange` item (0.147+, persisted for paginated threads, every local rollout's mode) and the older `patch_apply_end` (0.145–0.146, the same `changes`). Both come from Codex's patch handler after the patch ran, for a direct call and a nested one alike, whatever form the script built the patch in (literal, concatenation, variable). `turn_diff`, `patch_apply_begin` and `item_started` are never persisted; nothing is read from the app-server (row 9). |
| 2 | Where a recorded change shows | Keyed by the record's id. A direct `apply_patch` (0.145–0.151): its id is the call's, so it lands on the call's row and **replaces** the diffs read from the input. A script's nested patch: its id is `exec-<uuid>`, which no field links to the script (0 of 6,617 outputs carry one), so it is **a row of its own** named `apply_patch` (summary: its files) at its line, after the script and before the script's output (299 of 299 records sat inside an open script). Rejected: merging it into the script's row by position (a script runs commands, MCP calls and several patches; one row per patch keeps each status). One id is one row on re-read, paging, live updates and terminal switching (the index's `byID`). |
| 3 | Files, order, numbers | `add` → `create` (all `+`, from line 1), `delete` → `delete` (all `-` from the recorded content, which the patch text does not have), `update` → `edit` with the `unified_diff`'s hunks and their real `@@ -a,b +c,d @@` numbers (a count left out is 1; `\ No newline` lines kept), `move_path` → the new op **`rename`** with `to` (tag "renamed", "old → new" in the header; "Renamed only: no lines changed" for an empty diff). `changes` is a map (Rust's `HashMap`; Go's decoding loses order too): files are **sorted by path**. Rejected: the order of `stdout`'s list (its paths can be written differently from the map's keys). |
| 4 | A script's own text | Read only to show what it **proposes while it runs** (the shared view's "Proposed: not applied yet"): the plain string literals passed to `tools.apply_patch` (`"…"`, `'…'`, a template literal without `${…}`), decoded by hand: `\n \r \t \b \f \v \0`, `\xXX`, `\uXXXX`, `\u{…}`, surrogate pairs, line continuations and identity escapes (a real `\+` breaks a JSON decoder). Anything else (concatenation, a variable, `String.raw`, an object, a legacy octal escape, a raw line break) is **unread**: counted as `changes.computed` ("built by the script, not written out: the changes show once Codex applies it"), never guessed. The script is never run or evaluated, and is read **only when it has no `/` and no `\` in its code** (outside its strings' and templates' text; code inside `${…}` is code), so it has no comment, no regular expression and no escaped name, and where its strings and templates start and end is certain: regex-vs-division cannot be decided without a parser (automatic semicolon insertion: `var x` then a new line starting `/…/`), so the pass does not try. It also reads nothing from a script with `#`, a character outside ASCII or `<!--`/`-->` in its code, anything unterminated or a stray closer, `tools` used other than as `tools.…` (declared, a parameter, assigned, in a list), `with`, `tools.apply_patch` that is a member, constructed or not called, or `apply_patch` named any other way. **Any other script shows the computed note for every `apply_patch` it names**, never a guessed patch: a preview lost while it runs is cheap (Codex records what the patch did, row 1), an invented one is not. Coverage: 10 of the 13 `apply_patch`-bearing fixture scripts preview (the other 3 compute their patch, as before the rule), and 297 of 319 real 0.160–0.162 scripts calling `tools.apply_patch` (the rest compute their patch, or mention it only in a string, or (1) have a comment). Literal hunks have no line numbers (0) and are never shown as applied: the script's output, or the first record inside it, takes them away. |
| 5 | Dropping a running script's proposal | When the first record appears inside it (`linkScript`): the index is built in file order (the live tail and a rebuild alike, paging only reads indexed entries), so the newest `exec` without output is the script that ran it; scripts never overlapped in the survey (0 of 2,811 records with two open). Without this an interrupted script (no output ever) would keep its proposal next to the record. Cost: a script's second literal patch is no longer shown as proposed once its first is recorded. |
| 6 | Statuses | A record's `completed` is `ok`, `failed` `error` (its diffs stay: "may not have been applied, or only in part"; one real one, a script that caught the error and completed), `declined` `denied` (in source, never seen: synthetic fixture). The script's row has its own status from its output: "Script failed" is `error` (a patch that failed verification makes no record, so nothing else shows); "Script completed" says nothing of its patches. A direct `apply_patch` whose output is "apply_patch verification failed…" (20 real) is `error` with no diffs. Interrupted: turn-level only (`turn_aborted`): a script still running is interrupted (the shared rule), a record already made stays `ok`. |
| 7 | Large changes | As #171: a record's diffs go through the 64 KiB list cap (`cut`, "Show the whole change" through `transcript.item`); no line numbers are invented where a patch's text has none. |
| 8 | Subagents | Codex subagents (`agents.jsonl`, spawned threads) are not separate timelines in the view; their own rollouts would read the same way. Not otherwise affected. |
| 9 | App-server `fileChange` / `turn/diff/updated` | **Not wired.** Only shared-server workers have an app-server connection (`codexapp`), and it is live only: the persisted rollout is the durable source for every worker, history and paging. `fileChange` (`item/started` / `item/completed`, 0.162.1 schema `{id, status, changes: [{path, kind, diff}]}`, sorted by path) repeats the rollout's record under the same id; `item/fileChange/patchUpdated` streams a patch in flight; `outputDelta` is deprecated ("no longer emits"); `turn/diff/updated` is an aggregate of the whole turn, not per tool, apply_patch only, and invalidated to an empty string after an inexact or failed patch: adding it as a row would double every change. If live enrichment is ever added, dedupe by item id against the rollout's record and never show the turn diff as a call's change. |
| 10 | Coverage | Captured: direct and code-mode `apply_patch` (the only edits Codex records). **Not captured**: shell writes (`sed -i`, redirects, scripts, `git apply`, a `CommandExecution` records its command and output only), MCP tools that edit files (`McpToolCall` has a result, not changes), background processes, and edits in threads whose history mode drops the record. These show as the calls they are, with no change; nothing is derived from command text or a later `git diff` (cumulative, includes others' edits, misses untracked files). Capturing them would need before/after snapshots per call: a separate issue. A recorded change without lines (an empty `unified_diff`, a rename alone) says so ("No lines recorded", "Renamed only") instead of making some up. |

## 2026-10-15 — #49: Settings section navigation and a larger window

Settings was one 640 px column of 15 sections: finding a setting meant scrolling past the others. Now the window is a
workspace with one section at a time. Code: `game/src/ui/settings.ts` (`SettingsDialog`: `GROUPS`, `choose`, `show`,
`setUnavailable`, `fallBack`, `onTabKey`, `sync`), `settings.css` (the frame, `.settings-nav`, `.settings-tab`,
`.settings-pick`), the groups at the 15 `addSection` calls in `app.ts`; tests `src/ui/settings.dom.test.ts`,
`e2e/settings.spec.ts`, `e2e/h40-task-levels.spec.ts` and the `settingsSection` helper in `e2e/game.ts`.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Pattern | **Grouped vertical tabs, one section showing** (Carbon and PatternFly vertical tabs, the WAI-ARIA APG tabs pattern). Horizontal tabs would overflow with 15 labels; accordions and anchor links keep the long page. Search is left for later. |
| 2 | Groups | General (Appearance, Display, Day and night, Notifications), Workers (Harness models, Task levels, Codex workers), AI (AI integration and the five features on its models), Integrations (Repositories, Kaneo). A required `group` in `addSection`'s options (an unknown one throws), so the 15 call sites stay the one registry; the navigator lists the groups in that fixed order whatever the registration order. A unit test pins `app.ts`'s mapping to the issue's table. |
| 3 | Semantics | One `role="tablist"` (`aria-orientation="vertical"`, "Settings sections") owning only tabs; the group names are `aria-hidden` labels between them. Roving `tabindex`; Up / Down wrap, Home / End. **Automatic activation**: a switch only hides and shows DOM, it sends nothing and calls no section hook, so arrowing through is free. Panels are `role="tabpanel"` named by their tab; the inactive ones are `hidden`. |
| 4 | State across switches | Every section's DOM stays (no re-render): drafts, errors, disclosures and pending tests survive. A section's `open` hook still runs once per dialog open, never on a switch. Each section's scroll position in the shared content scroller is kept and restored. |
| 5 | Two kinds of hidden | `setHidden` (a bridge without the feature) is **unavailable**: no panel, tab or picker choice, its group label goes when the group is empty. Not showing is separate. When the section showing becomes unavailable, the next one in the navigator takes over (else the one before); the focus moves to the new tab only when it was in the section that went. |
| 6 | Which section an open shows | The first open: the first in the navigator (Appearance). Later opens in the page session: the last one chosen, when still offered (a fallback does not overwrite that memory). `reveal(id)` (Hire's model and task-level links, the handoff dialog, the AI features' "Set up in AI integration") overrides it: its target shows from the top with the heading focused, and becomes the remembered one. |
| 7 | Size | `min(1200px, 100vw − 32px)` × `min(90dvh, 100dvh − 32px)`, a fixed height so a switch never makes it jump (≈ 1200 × 691 at 1366 × 768, 1200 × 810 at 1440 × 900); the list is 224 px and scrolls by itself; a section is capped at 880 px for a readable measure. #40 holds: the dialog clips and never scrolls, only the list and the section do. |
| 8 | Narrow | **Below 800 px wide** (a 1366 × 768 laptop at 200 % zoom is 683 px) the list becomes a labelled native `<select>` with `<optgroup>`s above the section, in sync with the tabs (CSS shows one, so the other is out of the accessibility tree). On a phone (≤ 600 px) the window takes the screen less 8 px a side and the safe-area insets, the picker is 44 px tall; short and narrow (≤ 480 px tall), the picker's label sits beside it. |
| 9 | The plan-limits plate | **Settings is a screen for the corner** (`dialog.settings[open]` joins `SCREEN` in `ui/corner.ts`): at its new size the plate sat on its header and Close on a laptop. Over it the plate is the edge tab in the gutter beside it (83 px at 1366 px wide, 120 at 1440), the snug tab in a thinner one (from 1254 px wide, 1268 in the Ubuntu theme), away below that (Shift+L outside a text field still peeks: Settings, which keeps every other key from the game, lets that one out, `ui/settings.ts`); the inbox chip steps out with it. The About window stays small: the plate keeps above it. Test: the 1366 × 768 Settings journeys and the 1232 × 800 / 1366 × 768 Shift+L ones in `e2e/m10-limits.spec.ts`. |

## 2026-10-15 — #54: Repository discovery

`@r:` listed only what other floors were configured with. Now the bridge also scans folders on its host for Git
checkouts and merges them in, without duplicates. Code: `internal/repodisc` (walk, git lookups, identity, the
service), `internal/server/repodiscovery.go` and `repos.go` (`mergeRepos`), `internal/store/repodiscovery.go`,
`game/src/world/repoDiscovery.ts`, `game/src/laptop/repoSearch.ts`, `game/src/ui/repoDiscoverySection.ts`.
Research and measurements: `docs/research/repository-discovery-architecture.md`,
`docs/research/repository-scan-bench/README.md`.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Traversal | **Stop at each checkout boundary**: `Lstat <dir>/.git` (folder, file or link) before listing a folder; a marker makes it a candidate and the walk does not enter it. Every other folder is walked, at any depth, whatever its name: no depth cap and no dependency-name exclusions (the boundary already skips `node_modules` inside checkouts, which is where the cost was: `find -prune` took 8.7 s / >30 s on the two roots, the boundary walk 35 ms). Symlinked subfolders are not followed; explicit roots are resolved (`EvalSymlinks`), so an alias root is walked once. A repository nested in a checkout is found only from its own root, which is always walked even inside another root's checkout. Bare repositories are out of scope. The Settings section's "What is scanned" says so. |
| 2 | Git | Only for candidates, through two new allowlisted `gitops` shapes (`rev-parse --path-format=absolute --show-toplevel --git-common-dir`, `remote get-url origin`), 8 at once, 2 s each, no network. Every `gitops` command now also drops inherited `GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE` and the other repository-locating variables: a bridge started from a hook would otherwise point every `-C` call at one repository. Not `gitremote.Resolver`: it knows only GitHub, and discovery must list local-only and non-GitHub repositories. |
| 3 | Identity | A repository is the checkouts sharing a canonical Git common directory (worktrees) or a GitHub origin normalized by `gitremote.ParseGitHub` (ssh / https, case, `.git`). Nothing else merges: same-name folders, or two clones of one non-GitHub remote, stay apart (identity cannot be established safely). Its id is `gh:<owner/repo>` lowercased, else `git:<common dir>`. Checkouts in order: main checkouts (common dir is their own `.git`) first, then by path. |
| 4 | Merge with floors | Server-side, for **both** `worker.repos` and `hire.repos`, off the hub goroutine. A floor's identity is its `repo`; a folder-only floor's, the discovered repository of the checkout its folder is in (an `Lstat` walk up, symlinks resolved: subfolders and aliases match without a git call), else that checkout's origin, else the checkout, else the folder. One row per identity at the first floor's place. Winner: the first floor (floor order) with a usable folder, else the discovered checkout (main, then path), else the GitHub URL. The row keeps that floor as provenance and always carries the path it puts in (shown). Discovered-only repositories follow, by name. The all-buildings switch still governs only floor sources. |
| 5 | Row shape | `RepoRef` gains `id` (stable, opaque: the popup key) and `source` (`floor` / `discovered`), and `checkouts`; `session` is absent on a discovered row and `building` null. **No fake floor.** An older browser drops discovered rows (they have no `session`) and keeps floor rows; the new browser keys an older bridge's rows `floor:<session>`. |
| 6 | Service | Bridge-owned, wired by `app` with the bridge context (the `fileindex` precedent). `Configure` and `Refresh` never block (the hub calls them on its goroutine): a change of roots bumps a generation, cancels the scan in flight, drops removed roots' entries at once and asks for a scan; a result whose generation is old is thrown away, so a removed root never comes back. One scan at a time, bounded to a minute. Readers get an immutable `Catalog`; its `revision` moves only when its content does, so `repo_discovery.update`s for progress (at most every 250 ms) do not make a popup ask again. |
| 7 | Failures | Per root: a root that cannot be resolved or read, that runs past the bound, or where git answered for no candidate fails and **keeps its last successful result** (`stale`, with the error), never touching the other roots; never scanned successfully is `error`. A candidate git could not answer for (a timeout) keeps its previous entry; a failed origin lookup keeps the previous origin. Unreadable subfolders are skipped with a warning. |
| 8 | Settings | Their own table and messages (`repo_discovery_settings`, `repo_discovery.*`), not `repo_refs`: a separate section with its own validation and status, and `repo_refs` stays a one-field switch. Validation is syntactic (absolute, no line break, ≤ 4096 bytes, once after cleaning, ≤ 32 roots; 1–1440 minutes, default 10), checked by the browser first and again by the bridge (`bad_request` in words); existence is a scan result, so a temporarily unmounted root can be set. Without roots discovery is off. |
| 9 | Performance target | Warm full scan (walk + git validation + origin + identity merge) **under one second** on the two supplied roots; measured 151.5–168.9 ms median, p95 ≤ 176 ms, over four runs of 20 scans (`TestBenchRoots`, `docs/research/repository-scan-bench/production-results.jsonl`). No watcher: a 10-minute schedule plus Refresh now. |

## 2026-10-15 — #57: Several bridges, switched from ⌘P

The helper tunnelled to the one host `-host` named. It now keeps a list of named bridges, Settings → Bridges edits it
and ⌘P → Switch bridge picks one. The ticket left two questions open; the rest of the decisions are its Proposal's.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "Online": standby tunnels or periodic probes? | **One supervised connection per bridge, all the time**: an ssh entry its own `Tunnel` ending at `<dir of -local-socket>/bridges/<id>-<n>.sock` (row 11) (the existing supervisor: backoff to 30 s, `/healthz` every 5 s, `ControlMaster=no`), a local entry its socket dialled directly and checked every 5 s. The palette and Settings read a cached state, never an ssh handshake, and a switch to a bridge that is up is instant. The price is one idle `ssh -N` per ssh bridge, a handful with a handful of bridges, and an unreachable one costs a failed ssh start per backoff step (30 s at most). Periodic short-lived probes would save the idle processes but make every switch a fresh handshake and the state up to a probe interval old; they stay the fallback if idle ssh turns out to be unwanted. |
| 2 | `h3d-helper -bridge <name>` for scripting? | **Not now**: the game switches through `/api/bridges`; a script can too (`curl` with the token header). |
| 3 | Where the list lives | **The helper**, `bridges.json` (0600, its directory 0700) next to the token: `<config>/h3d-helper/bridges.json` (`~/Library/Application Support/h3d-helper/` on the Mac), `-bridges-file` / `$H3D_HELPER_BRIDGES_FILE` for tests and repros. Not a bridge's (unreachable while it is down) nor the browser's (the helper acts on it). |
| 4 | `-host` once there is a list | **The file is the truth once it exists.** `-host` / `$H3D_HELPER_HOST` (with `-remote-socket`) seed a missing or empty list (the host, named after it, in use: an install from before #57 comes up as it was) and are ignored after that, with a warning naming the file when they name a host it does not have. Otherwise the LaunchAgent's variable would undo every switch at the next restart. |
| 5 | `-local-socket` | Kept, so an installed plist's `H3D_HELPER_LOCAL_SOCKET` still works: its **directory** anchors the tunnels' local ends, `<dir>/bridges/<id>-<n>.sock`. Ids are 10 hex digits and `<n>` is checked as six base-36 digits, so the Mac's `~/Library/Caches/h3d-helper/bridges/<id>-<n>.sock` stays far below the 103-byte limit; an add whose path would not fit is refused in words. |
| 6 | Discovery of a remote socket | It used to run once at start and stop the helper on failure. Now it runs in the bridge's own supervisor, with backoff and the error as that bridge's `last_error`: it never holds up the start or another bridge. |
| 7 | What a switch closes | **Every connection dialled to the old bridge**, not only `/ws`: the proxies' `http.Transport`s keep idle keep-alive connections, and one left open would carry the next `/logo` or `/whiteboard` request to the old bridge. A dial that finishes after the switch is closed and refused. |
| 8 | `GET /api/bridges` guard | `/api/open`'s, but an **absent** `Origin` passes on `GET` (as on `/logo`): a browser sends none on a same-origin `GET`. The `X-H3D-Token` header still makes it a request no other site can send. Changes need the `Origin`. |
| 9 | The per-bridge key scope at start | The game asks the helper which bridge is in use before the App reads any key (in parallel with the font load, 2.5 s at most; the helper answers from memory). When it does not answer, the id it last named (`h3d.bridges.active`) is used, so a draft never lands in another bridge's keys. The keys from before #57 move into the **first** bridge's namespace, once (`h3d.bridges.migrated`). |
| 10 | Removing the bridge in use | Refused by the helper (`409 in_use`) and by Settings (the button says why; it asks no one). The list is never empty while it has a bridge in use. |
| 11 | Editing a bridge's address | **A fresh tunnel on a socket of its own, at once**: each tunnel the helper starts ends at `<id>-<n>.sock`, `<n>` counting the tunnels of this helper run. Review round 1 found that one `<id>.sock` per bridge, with the new tunnel waiting for the old one to stop, was not enough: the new tunnel was in use (and dialled) before it waited, so a dial during the old ssh's shutdown (up to `KillGrace`) reached the **old** host; and a second edit cancelled the waiting one, whose wait ended at once, so a third tunnel could start while the first still held the path, and the first's cleanup then unlinked the third's socket. A path per tunnel removes the shared resource: a dial reaches the new tunnel or nothing, and an old tunnel's cleanup only ever removes its own socket. Sockets an earlier helper run left (killed before its tunnels cleaned up) are removed when the list starts, but only those nothing listens on (the connection is refused) and never a local bridge's own path: a name alone does not make a socket stale (review round 2 found a live local bridge's socket in the socket directory unlinked). Tests: `TestAddressEditNeverReachesTheOldHost`, `TestRapidAddressEditsKeepTheirSockets`, `TestStaleTunnelSocketsGo`, `TestStartupKeepsLiveSockets`. |
| 12 | Another tab switches | **The helper refuses a page's `/ws` for another bridge than its own, and the page reloads on the new one.** Review round 1: a switch reloaded only the tab that made it; another tab's `/ws` was closed, reconnected through the new bridge and applied its snapshot while its drafts, seats and overview stores kept the old bridge's scope (and wrote it). Relabelling is not enough, since the scope is chosen once at start, before the stores read their keys. The page's `/ws` names its bridge (`?bridge=<id>`, `none` with none); `BridgeSet.Dial` compares it with the bridge in use under its lock, the post-dial check and `activate` closing the old bridge's connections cover a switch during the dial, and the `/ws` transport keeps no idle connections, so every `/ws` is checked. The page learns of the switch from any list it gets (polls while Settings or the switch list is open) and, since a background tab polls nothing, by asking for the list whenever its connection drops; on a mismatch it reloads with "Switching to <name>…" (`followActiveBridge`). Chosen over checking `/api/bridges` before each reconnect alone: that leaves a window between the check and the dial; the helper's check has none. The HTTP routes (`/logo`, `/upload`, `/whiteboard`, `/screen/frame`, `/drops`) are scoped the same way (review round 2: a stale tab's whiteboard autosave reached the new bridge, which took its scene on a matching floor name and base version): their URLs derive from the `/ws` one and carry `bridge`; their guard refuses another bridge with `409 bridge_changed` before proxying and hands the expectation to `Dial` for a switch made after the check; and since these proxies keep idle connections, the proxied URL's host names the page's bridge, so a proxy's pool never hands a page of A a connection dialled for B. A route's `bridge_changed` sends the page to the same follow path (it asks for the list and reloads). Tests: `TestWSKeepsThePagesBridge`, `TestRoutesKeepThePagesBridge`, `TestStaleWhiteboardSaveIsRefused`, `net/bridgeFollow.test.ts`, `net/bridgeChanged.test.ts`, the e2e "a switch made in another tab" and "a request the helper refuses for a switch made elsewhere". |

## 2026-10-15 — #68: Bash is the default bridge shell, Fish an explicit option

v1.45.0 hardcoded `/bin/fish` for a floor's wake and `setsid -f fish <launcher>` (a `.fish` file) for the shared Codex
server, a host-specific assumption carried over from the research machine (`docs/research/herdr-followup.md` §2a): a
Bash-login host (Omarchy) had to install Fish just to wake a floor. This entry **supersedes** the Fish-only wording of
the #155 rows 2 and 4 above (and of the research notes); those rows stay as the record of what v1.45.0 did.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The policy's shape | **A two-value enum, not a command**: `--shell bash\|fish` / `HERDR_WORLD_SHELL` on the bridge, next to `--codex-run`, default `bash`, validated at startup (`internal/shell`). No arbitrary command, no per-floor setting. |
| 2 | What may select Fish | **Only the option.** `$SHELL` is never read, and neither an installed fish nor an inherited `SHELL=…/fish` changes anything. Bash stays the default on a host with fish. |
| 3 | Finding the shell | The policy's absolute path: `$PATH`, then `/usr/bin`, `/bin`, `/usr/local/bin` (a user systemd manager's `PATH` is short). Fish prefers `/bin/fish` so its wake argv stays byte-identical to v1.45.0's, and is typed as a bare `fish` for the launcher as before. A path typed into a pane must be plain characters. Resolved at startup; a missing shell is an error naming the option and both fixes. The cached path is checked again before every wake and launch (it still exists, is a regular file after symlinks, has an execute bit: `shell.Verify`), because the detached `setsid -f` of a launch discards its exit status and the bridge would wait for a socket that never appears: the same error is returned and nothing is dispatched if the shell vanishes later. **Never a silent switch** to the other shell. |
| 4 | Launcher naming | **`<marker hex>.sh` for bash, `.fish` for fish.** `.sh` because it is short (the 90-byte path rule and the 108-byte socket path leave little room) and conventional; it is always run as `<abs bash> <file>`, never as `sh`, so Bash syntax is safe. `Control` refuses a launcher of the other format than its policy; the placeholder rules (`[A-Za-z0-9._/-]`, in `CodexRun`) are unchanged. |
| 5 | Upgrade and recovery | **`Launch.Remove` deletes the launcher of every shell's format** and `Write` replaces any. A stored launch has no shell (its record is unchanged): a `.fish` launch written by v1.45.0 is stopped, recovered and cleaned up by a bash-default bridge. A launch is only ever started afresh, never by re-running an old launcher. |
| 6 | The Bash launcher and wrappers | A non-interactive `bash script` reads no `~/.bashrc`, so a user's `codex` function or alias would be lost silently, where fish reads `config.fish` even then. The launcher therefore exports the marker, records `$$`, and **`exec`s an interactive bash** (`"$BASH" -i +H -c '<command>'`): `~/.bashrc` is read as in the pane (behind the `$- != *i*` guard), the pane's exports are inherited, no terminal is needed. Chosen over sourcing `~/.bashrc` from a script (the guard returns before the function) and over `bash -i <script>` (the pid file then holds a process whose own environment lacks the marker: exports of a running shell do not change `/proc/<pid>/environ`). Checked empirically under real bash with a `.bashrc` function and alias, and in a real throwaway herdr pane. |
| 7 | The pid contract | The pid file holds the launcher process, now the `exec`'d interactive bash: it stays the supervisor (a `codex` function runs as its child), leads its own session (`setsid`) and **its own environment carries `H3D_CODEX_SERVER`**. Ownership is still the marker scan (`MarkedPIDs`, `FindServer`, `Alive`): the supervisor's command line holds the quoted `--listen '…'`, so only the codex process is found as the server. `HISTFILE` is unset in the launch, so the user's history is never rewritten. |
| 8 | What Bash wrappers support | `codex` functions and aliases in `~/.bashrc` (also `command codex`). Not: a `.bashrc` that execs another shell or needs the terminal, and functions or aliases only a login file or prompt hook defines. Exported variables (direnv's, `PATH`, `HERDR_*`) always arrive. Documented in `docs/protocol.md` "Shell policy" and `CONTRIBUTING.md` "Bash and Fish hosts". |
| 9 | Unchanged | `CleanEnv`, argument validation, 0700/0600 permissions, ownership verification, "stop only that worker's server", `--working-directory`, `KillMode=process`, the unit name. |
| 10 | Tests | `TestControlAllowlist` runs both policies; real-bash launcher tests (`internal/codexapp`) cover the wrapper sentinel for function, alias and fish, the marker, pid, detached session, history, quoting and cleanup of both formats; the integration fixture takes `H3D_CODEXSHARE_SHELL` (bash default; fish skipped when absent). |
## 2026-10-15 — #58 (stage 1): Add floor → New herdr session

Starting a project meant creating a herdr session outside the game. Now the city's Add floor (top bar, and the
Buildings panel's lot) starts one: `session.create`. Code: `internal/herdr/newsession.go`, `internal/server/sessioncreate.go`,
`game/src/ui/addFloor.ts`, `game/src/world/addFloor.ts`. Research: the run's `ground-herdr-sess.md` (herdr 0.9.3 on the
bridge host, every name and timing below measured there).

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | How is a session created? | herdr has no `session create`: a server started under a name herdr has not seen creates it. A **new allowlisted shape** `create-session` with the wake's exact argv (`systemd-run --user --unit=herdr-s-{session} … exec herdr --session '{session}' server`), so the tripwire (`TestControlAllowlist`) records the intent separately from waking, and `Control.CreateSession` refuses a name that fails the new-name rule before anything runs. It reuses `{session}`, so `Control.Only` (`--sessions`) applies. No new executable: the unit check below reads a file. |
| 2 | Which names? | `herdr.CheckNewSessionName`: `^[a-z0-9_-]+$` (decision 1 of the issue; herdr would also take uppercase and dots), no leading `-` (herdr takes it, every argv would read it as a flag), not `default`, and **the host's `sun_path` cap**: herdr binds `<sessions folder>/<name>/herdr-client.sock`, which must fit 108 bytes, so the cap is `108 − len(sessions folder) − 1 − len("/herdr-client.sock")` (56 on `/home/jeff`, 53 on `/home/jeffrey`; measured 56 accepted, 57 fails with herdr's "exceeds capacity of sun_path"), never above herdr's 64. The sessions folder is learned from `session list` (a named session's parent, else `sessions/` in the default's), never a constant. `ValidSessionName` stays as it was: existing sessions may have dots or uppercase. The browser checks the same rule (minus the host cap) as it is typed; the bridge's words come back for the rest. |
| 3 | When is a name taken? | Starting a known name **wakes** that session, so "free" means free everywhere herdr looks: not a floor, not in `herdr session list` in **any** state (asleep sessions and `default` included), nothing at `<sessions folder>/<name>` (stale folders, backups), and no loaded transient unit `herdr-s-<name>.service` (its unit file under `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/systemd/transient/`, `/run/user/<uid>` when the variable is unset, which systemd removes when `--collect` unloads it). `name_taken` with the reason. |
| 4 | `--sessions` | Off in production (no `HERDR_WORLD_SESSIONS` on the deployed unit), so rule 2 is the real gate. When set, a name it does not match is `bad_request` before anything runs: the floor would never show. |
| 5 | Not through `floorFor` | The floor does not exist yet, so `session.create` has its own path: checks on the hub goroutine (args, name format, `--sessions`, a floor by the name, a create in flight), the rest (`session list`, cap, taken, start, wait) off it. The ack comes once the watcher saw the floor running (`applyFloor` signals the create after the floor's `floor.upsert` went out), so a client can `floor.assign` it at once; the create asks for a rescan every 250 ms meanwhile, since herdr lists the session ~90 ms after the start. |
| 6 | Concurrency | One create per name: a second one while the first runs is `busy`; afterwards the name is taken. Different names run side by side. |
| 7 | Failure | Bounded by `--wake-timeout` (10 s). If `systemd-run` fails or the floor is not seen running, the bridge stops the session (`herdr session stop`) and removes `<sessions folder>/<name>` — only that folder, which step 3 proved absent before the call. `session_create_failed` carries herdr's error verbatim. |
| 8 | The dialog | A desk dialog (`desk-dlg`) listing the kinds of floor as the send-home dialog's radio list, with only "New herdr session" now: the virtual floor of stage 4 is a second entry in `FLOOR_KINDS`, not a new dialog. On success the Buildings panel opens on the new floor's Move menu, so assigning a building is the existing `floor.assign`. The bridge's refusals of the name go to the field, herdr's failures to the footer; a result after the dialog was closed is a toast. |

## 2026-10-15 — #58 (stage 2): virtual floors on the bridge

A floor was a herdr session. Now a session has its **real floor** (floor id = session name) and any number of
**virtual floors** (`v-<12 hex>`) the user defines on it, each with its own desks, names, settings, board, whiteboard
and handoffs; the bridge's store says which floor a worker is on (protocol: "Virtual floors"). Code:
`internal/store/vfloor.go`, `internal/server/vfloors.go`. This entry is the behaviour on top of the data model
(the issue's decisions 1–4 are binding and not repeated).

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What names a floor's folders? | Its **floor id**, not a session name (supersedes the 2026-10-01 M13 row 6 rule). A real floor's id is its session's name, so existing whiteboards and handoffs stay where they are; a virtual floor's `v-<12 hex>` is itself a valid session name, so `whiteboard.ValidFloor` and `handoff.FloorDir` are unchanged (`TestFloorIDsAreSessionNamesOrVirtualIDs`). A handoff is its worker's floor's (`Source.Floor`, the cabinet, the counts); `handoff.added` / `handoff.removed` carry it as `floor_id` with `session` its session (C2a's rule for floor events), so a pre-#58 client reading `session` still counts a real floor's handoffs right. The LLM handoff's prompt names the floor by its display name. |
| 2 | Decision 3: how does a hire wake the session? | Through the wake itself, not a copy: `floor.wake`'s `beginWake` / `runWake` with a continuation (`floorOp.then`) that runs on the hub goroutine instead of the wake's reply, then `worker.hire` / `run.start` go on as on a running floor. The wake is audited as its own `floor.wake` row (args name the command and its `req_id`). The "Waking floor…" stage is the floor's existing `transition: "waking"` (every floor of the session shows it), not a new event: the client already shows transitions. Cheap argument checks run before the wake so a request that cannot be a hire wakes nothing. `hire.prepare` / `run.prepare` answer on an asleep virtual floor (the dialogs must open there). Real floors keep `invalid_state`: decision 3 is about virtual floors, and an asleep real floor has its own Wake. A wake failure fails the hire with `wake_failed` and the wake's words. |
| 3 | The hidden setter | A dedicated `floor.hide {floor, hidden}`, not a `floor.settings.set` key: settings are the board/repository/hire record (`floor_repo`, with its Kaneo check and "clear the row when empty" rule), while hidden is a view flag with its own table (`floor_hidden`). Any floor in the world, or a defined virtual floor whose session is away. |
| 4 | `floor.delete` with `move_workers` | One store transaction re-attributes every stored worker of the floor (open ones too) to the base's real floor and drops the floor's rows; then the hub re-applies the session's last observation (asleep too), so the real floor seats them at its lowest free desks and names them like newcomers, through the same path a deleted floor's stray worker takes. A hire in flight on the floor makes it `busy` (its worker is not stored yet). Events: `floor.removed`, then the workers' `worker.upsert` on the real floor. |
| 5 | The deleted floor's whiteboard and handoffs | **Kept.** Deleting a definition never deletes files: the user's drawings and handoffs stay on disk under the `v-…` folder (an id is never minted again, so nothing else lands there). The handoffs stay listed under that floor id. |
| 6 | Integration tests' hires | A hire's attribution does not need an agent: the integration test's bridge hires through a `HireHerdr` that is herdr's own `Control` but starts no agent (`ServeConfig.Hire`, nil in production), so the workspace, its pane marker, the herdr restart and the bridge restart are all real while no Claude or Codex runs. |
| 7 | Which floor is virtual? | The one with a stored definition (`virtual_floor`, the hub's floor state; the client's `Floor.virtual`), never an id's shape: `store.IsVirtualFloorID` only checks an id being minted. The shape `v-<12 hex>` is **reserved**: `herdr.CheckNewSessionName` refuses it, so `session.create` never makes a session in the virtual ids' namespace (the client's name check says so as it is typed). A real session that already has such a name (made outside the game) is a real floor and a base like any other. |

## 2026-10-15 — #58 (stage 3): freelancers on their orchestrator's floor

Freelancers are made by the orchestrator's agent with plain `herdr`, so no hire records their floor. They take their
orchestrator's floor through the run link (`runs.Links`), and the bridge stores it (protocol: "Virtual floors",
Freelancers). Code: `internal/server/runs.go` (`inheritFloors`), called from `syncWorkers`.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which floor counts as given, never to be overridden? | A worker is **attributed** when the bridge hired it (`hired_at`, on whichever floor) or its stored floor is a virtual floor. No new column: a stored real floor of an unhired worker is what the store writes for a workspace nothing placed (migration 26 backfilled every row so, and a freelancer is stored before its `h3d_run` token arrives), so it must stay open to inheritance, or the common case (seen, stored, then linked) would never move. Inheriting the real floor therefore stores nothing new and stays open, which is harmless: the real floor is the fallback anyway. A worker `floor.delete` moved to the real floor is likewise open again and follows its orchestrator. |
| 2 | Where does inheritance happen? | In `syncWorkers`, between resolving each worker's stored floor and fanning the session's workers out to its floors, with links computed once over the whole session (`sessionMembers`: this observation's workers on their tentative floors, other sessions' floors from their views). So the freelancer is on its orchestrator's floor in the first `worker.upsert` after its link resolves (no flash on the real floor), and `persist` stores the floor with the worker as any changed floor. Only a floor of the freelancer's own session: `runs.Links` may link across sessions, and a workspace never leaves its session. |
| 3 | The orchestrator's floor | Its `run_launch.floor_id` (`runs.Launch.Floor`) while that names a floor, else the floor it is on. The two agree today (the hire records the same floor); the record names a deleted floor after `floor.delete` with `move_workers`, which moved the orchestrator to the real floor. A later `worker.move_floor` (stage 5) must decide whether a moved orchestrator's record follows it. `storeRunLaunch` now answers for every launch record (a text ticket's too), with no issue or item when it names none; `runs.Links` reads only those. |
| 4 | `h3d_floor` | Honoured only on a worker nothing attributed and that no run link placed, and only when it names a floor in the world on the workspace's session; then stored like any attribution (a later token does not move it). The orchestrator's first prompt asks for it (a line after the placement line, naming the run's floor id, on real floors too); the write-code skill never mentions floors, so it stays usable outside herdr-3d. It is a hint: the link is the mechanism, and the token is gone after a herdr restart. |

## 2026-10-15 — #58 (stage 4): Settings → Floors and Add floor's virtual floor

The floors are managed in the game: Settings → Floors lists them and Add floor makes either kind. Code:
`game/src/ui/floorsSection.ts`, `game/src/world/floorList.ts`, `game/src/ui/addFloor.ts`, `game/src/world/addFloor.ts`.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Where does the section's list come from? | The world the client already holds (`floor.upsert` / `floor.removed` and the workers), not a new snapshot key: `FloorListBook` in the store re-reads it after every change that moves floors or workers and tells the section only when its rows changed, so another client's add, rename, hide or delete shows at once and a status change costs nothing. A virtual floor whose session is away is not in the world, so it is not listed until the session comes back (the lede says so). The section sits under General: floors are the city itself, not one integration. |
| 2 | Delete | In place under the row, like the presets' confirmation: no extra modal over Settings. It names the workers on the floor and where they go; with any, the button says "Move N workers and delete" and sends `move_workers: true`, never silently. The bridge also counts hires in flight and an away session's stored workers: a plain delete it refuses with `invalid_state` shows its words and offers the move. |
| 3 | Hide | A Show / Hide button on every row (decision 2) with one explanation under the list: a hidden floor leaves the lists while nobody works on it and shows again when a worker is on it. A hidden floor that shows because of its workers says who. The floor you stand on stays listed too (`WorldStore.pin`: the floor whose interior you are in, however you got there — the elevator, the stairs, a pose; none in the lobby or the city; on the fire escape and the street the last one, so the building does not change shape under the stairs), so a hide or its last worker leaving never takes it away under you, and a `?floor=` link opens a hidden floor. Should the floor with the interior still drop out of its building's stack, the walk lists it again rather than move you to no storey. |
| 4 | Add floor's second choice | `FLOOR_KINDS` gains "Virtual floor on an existing session": a free-text name (trimmed, 1–64 bytes, no control characters, checked as typed) in the body face instead of the session name's mono, and a base session picked from the real floors (asleep and hidden ones too; the session of the floor you are on first). Never a building (decision 4). From Settings it opens on the virtual kind and its result is the section's status line; from the city a toast says which building the floor stands in. |

## 2026-10-16 — #60: grill rounds keep every question, choice and recommendation

A ten-question grill round (Q8–Q17) became one fallback "Open question": the extraction's 1,200-token budget went
entirely on reasoning, and a valid answer would still have been cut to 8 questions with no recommendations. The round
is now read whole, the LLM call has room, and the card answers it question by question (protocol: "Follow-up
questions"). Code: `internal/followup/round.go` (the reader), `internal/followup/followup.go` (the LLM path),
`internal/server/followup.go` (retry, message, the prompt), `game/src/laptop/followupCard.ts`.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Parser or model for grill rounds? | **Parser first.** A message whose ❓ blocks are all `❓ **Qn** <sep> **Title**[:] body` or `❓ **Qn <sep> Title[.:]** body` is authoritative (`source: "parsed"`, no LLM call): label, title, body as Markdown context, lettered options (bullets, else inline `(a) … (b) …`), else plain bullets none of which asks a `?` (a bullet that asks is a sub-question: context, the question open; options are never invented), and the `➡️` paragraph as the recommendation, which names an option when it starts with its letter. Any other ❓ line (outside a code fence) sends the whole message to the LLM rather than dropping a block. Fixtures: the incident's round (`internal/followup/grill_sample.md`, byte-exact, also `followups.test`'s sample), its first round, and a synthesized open round; `game/fixtures/followup-grill.json` is the reader's golden output. |
| 2 | How many questions, and over that? | The reader takes 30, the LLM 16. Over the bound, and every question the LLM reports in `more` or that is invalid, is counted in `Followup.unlisted`; the card says how many are only in the message and offers it whole. Never a silent drop, never the whole round rejected for count. |
| 3 | One invalid question from the LLM | Bounded repair: an empty, over-long or unknown-kind question is left out and counted; a choice with bad options becomes open with its options in the context; context is cut at 1,200 characters, `why` at 600 (a recommendation: decision 10). Only an answer with no valid question (or no JSON) is the fallback. |
| 4 | Budget, effort, timeout | `ExtractMaxTokens = 8192`, effort `low` only when the model's catalog says it has reasoning and lists `low` (an endpoint the catalog does not describe gets no effort, so a plain self-hosted model is never sent one), `NoPartial`, no `BudgetWins` (the follow-up has no budget setting). Cut off or empty: once more with `none` (else `minimal`), then the fallback. A per-call `Timeout` of **45 s** with `NoUncertainRetry`: the measured `low` calls took 13.8–19.7 s, over the shared 15 s, and a timed-out call is not paid twice. The game waits 200 s for `followups.test` and 100 s for a retry. |
| 5 | Measurement (`openai/gpt-6-luna` via OpenRouter, the incident's round through the LLM path, `go test ./internal/followup -run Live`, 12 calls) | First prompt: `low`@8192 ×3: 1,793/1,897/2,059 completion tokens of which 404/460/584 reasoning, 13.8/14.5/16.1 s, all `stop`, but 10, **12** and 12 questions (sub-bullets split out); `none`@8192 ×2: one `{"questions":[]}` (11 tokens, 1.1 s), one 10/10 in 13.5 s; the provider default (`medium`)@8192: 3,682 tokens, 2,214 reasoning, 33.7 s, 14 questions; `low`@4096: 1,965 tokens, 585 reasoning, 16.2 s, 10/10. With the prompt rule "one question per number, sub-questions in its context": `low`@8192 ×4: 1,938/1,831/1,742/1,825 completion tokens, 477/414/366/380 reasoning (≤ 6 % of the budget), 19.1/19.3/17.6/19.7 s, **10 of 10 questions with their recommendations every time**; `none`@8192: 1,576 tokens, 14.0 s, 10/10. The last `low` answer is `internal/followup/testdata/grill-round2-llm.json`, the fakes' grill answer. The 4,096 floor would fit, 8,192 leaves room for a longer round. |
| 6 | Model check | `followups.test` adds `grill`, the incident's round, through the classifier and then `ExtractLLM` (never the reader): it passes only when it is flagged and the LLM lists all 10, each with a recommendation. The Settings panel sums it up in a line instead of listing ten questions. |
| 7 | Fallback recovery | The follow-up carries `error` (why) and `retries`. The bridge keeps the message in memory with the open follow-up (the transcript gives it back after a restart): `worker.followup_message` answers it on request, never in an upsert. `worker.retry_followup` runs the extraction only, for a fallback, at most 3 times, one at a time, and records the result in `followup_seen`, so a restart restores the recovered round. Nothing retries by itself (no poll, no restart). |
| 8 | Answers | Paired by label: `- Q9 <question> → <answer>`; an answer that is the recommendation as it was gets ` (your recommendation)`; `Unanswered: Q12, Q15` lists the rest; an optional round `note` ends the prompt. A round without labels keeps `n. <question> → <answer>` (plus the Unanswered line). Values and notes may be 8,000 bytes (an accepted recommendation is copied whole, decision 10); the whole prompt is still bound by herdr's 16 KiB (all ten of the incident's recommendations accepted fit; a round whose accepted answers go over is refused with `bad_request`, which the card shows, never cut). |
| 9 | The card | Per question: its label, its context rendered (the transcript's Markdown rules: marked + DOMPurify), its options with the recommended one marked, the recommendation with **Use recommendation** (R), its own answer. **Use all recommendations** fills only the unanswered (yours stay). Nothing fills-and-sends: Send / Ctrl+Enter is the only way, Enter in a text moves on, R in a text is a letter. The foot says how many are answered and which go as unanswered. A fallback says the questions could not be prepared and why, offers Retry and the whole message, and keeps the free answer: after a retry reads the questions it becomes the round's note. Picks and the note are kept per worker and turn (`FollowupDrafts`, storage where allowed) across a retry, a reconnect, standing up and a reload. |
| 10 | A long recommendation | **Whole up to an answer's bound, else shown and never accepted.** Review found both readers cut `recommendation.text` at 1,200 characters with `…`, and Use recommendation then sent that start as the agent's recommendation (a 1,446-byte one lost its last sentence, "Never delete the existing production database."). The bound is now in bytes and is the answer's: `followup.MaxRecommendationLen` = 8,000 = `proto.MaxFollowupAnswer` (one constant, so they cannot drift). Up to it the text is kept whole by the reader and by `Parse`; one 8,000-byte answer with its label, question (≤ 300 characters) and the marker stays under herdr's 16 KiB. Over it, the text is its first 8,000 bytes (never inside a character) with `cut: true`: the card shows it with a line pointing at **Show the whole message**, offers no Use recommendation (nor R, nor Use all for it), and the bridge never adds ` (your recommendation)` to a cut text, even typed out. **The bridge refuses a cut text sent as an answer itself** (review round 3, the user's decision): a client that drops `cut` (the branch's own earlier one did) offered it as Use recommendation, and the bridge acked the 8,000-byte start of an 8,446-byte recommendation without its last sentence. `worker.answer_followup` is now `bad_request` ("Q12's recommendation was too long to send whole: read the whole message and write your answer") for a value or note that is the cut text with or without its `…`, its spaces and line breaks folded; nothing goes to the worker and the follow-up stays open. A typed answer that differs, and a named option, are sent. A choice whose cut recommendation names an option still offers the option: the option is what is sent, whole. The LLM budget still holds: 8,192 tokens is ~32 KB of JSON against the round's measured ~1.8k tokens, and an 8,000-byte recommendation is ~2k tokens; an answer cut off at the budget goes to the retry and then the fallback (decision 4), never a silently short recommendation. |

## 2026-10-16 — Codex compatibility preference and 0.163.0

Codex upgrades should not force a player who accepts compatibility risk to wait for another herdr-3d release. The
working model stays one Codex terminal managed inside Herdr, with the bridge following its conversation and tool
steps in the transcript.

- **Settings → Codex workers → Allow unverified Codex versions** is a separate, bridge-wide preference, off by
  default and stored in SQLite. It persists across bridge restarts and future upgrades. The UI names the risk:
  transcript updates or question replies may stop working on an untested release. Codex 0.163 is added to the
  verified set, so it does not need this opt-in.
- The same policy covers CLI preflight, the actual server's handshake on hire/wake, and thread connections.
  Changing it refreshes bridge connections; it does not relaunch terminals, servers or turns. Existing legacy
  workers keep their launch mode. The opt-in only relaxes the release allowlist: command failures, malformed
  versions, blocked environments, process ownership and thread identity checks still apply.
- `codex_server.set` patches either preference, preserving omitted fields. An old client's launch-switch change
  cannot reset the risk preference. Older bridges omit `allow_unverified`, so the new game hides that control.
- A Codex waiting at **Hooks need review** can make Herdr 0.9.3 return `timeout` or `agent_name_not_found` and drop
  its pending agent name. The adapter recognizes that complete dialog after those errors, restores the requested
  name on the existing pane, and returns the existing recoverable `agent_not_ready` result. The hire holds its first
  prompt for the player. It neither approves hooks nor starts a second terminal. This covers both shared and
  embedded Codex launches and their resume paths; other startup errors retain their normal handling.

Verification and upstream details are in [Codex shared TUI research §15](research/codex-shared-tui.md#15-codex-01630).

## 2026-10-16 — #62 (part 1 of #61): the bridge's schedule store and runner

Agents end turns with work that can only happen later ("24h soak, until about 10:45 UTC tomorrow; then delete the
backups"). The bridge now stores one-shot schedules and, when one is due, hires a fresh worker with its prompt, with
no client connected (protocol: "Scheduled agents"). Code: `internal/store/schedule.go`, `internal/server/schedule.go`,
`internal/proto/schedule.go`, `handoff.HireTask`, `world.Listed`. The game's dialog and boards are parts 3 and 4.

| # | Question | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | One code path for a hire | **The fire is `worker.hire`'s hire**: `beginHire` / `runHire` / `finishHire`, unchanged in what they do. A schedule passes the bridge's own `Client` identity (`bridge:schedule`: the audit log's client; never connected, so its replies go nowhere) and a `hireWatch`: `begun` writes `running` with the minted worker id before anything reaches herdr (an error refuses the hire), `settled` ends the schedule from `finishHire`. No copy of the hire's checks, caps or rollback. Clients see a bridge hire exactly like a game's: its `floor.upsert`, `worker.upsert`, statuses. |
| 2 | The clock | **The wall clock, looked at**: every 30 s, once on startup when discovery's first `world.Listed` says the floors are known, and when a floor is first seen. Never a `time.Timer` to the due time: a monotonic timer stops while the host is suspended. `Options.Now` is the clock, so every test runs on an injected one. |
| 3 | Missed window | A schedule first **seen** due within `due_at + 5 min` fires normally (two missed 30 s looks and a slow restart still fit). Past it: `skip` → `skipped` "missed: bridge was offline at <due> (seen <now>)"; `run_late` (default) over `max_lateness` → `skipped` naming the lateness; else it fires with the late notice first in its prompt ("This task was scheduled for <due UTC> and is running at <now UTC>, <dur> late. Check whether anything below is stale before acting."). `seen_at` keeps when it was first seen, so a schedule that then waits (a waking floor) is not judged again. Run now and retry skip the policy; a retry past the grace still gets the notice. |
| 4 | Once, across restarts | `running` is written before herdr is called. On startup a `running` schedule is `done` if the bridge ever recorded its worker (the `worker` table, closed or not), else `failed` "bridge stopped while hiring". Never fired twice. |
| 5 | When the floor cannot take it | Floor gone (discovery no longer lists its session, or a deleted virtual floor) → `failed`. Asleep: `wake_floor` (default on) wakes it through `wakeFor`, the path a hire on an asleep virtual floor already took (#58), audited as the bridge's `floor.wake`; the schedule is `waiting` and fires when the wake ends; a failed wake fails it. Off → `failed` "floor asleep: wake_floor is off". Waking or sleeping (`busy`), not seen yet, or no free desk → `waiting`, tried on the next look. A floor is "gone" only once discovery has listed the sessions: before that the bridge does not know. |
| 6 | Desk, cwd, caps | The bridge picks the **first free desk** (no worker holds it, no hire on its way) within the floor's desks; the hire grows the floor as any hire does. `cwd` missing → `failed` "working directory <cwd> no longer exists", never another directory. A hard cap → `failed` with the hire's message, which names `--max-workers-per-floor` / `--max-workers`. |
| 7 | The prompt and its handoff | The schedule's prompt, then the handoff line in the cabinet's Hire from handoff wording, from one Go helper (`handoff.HireTask`; `TestHireTaskIsTheGames` reads `game/src/world/handoff.ts` and checks the template is the same text). A deleted handoff: "The handoff <path> this task referenced was deleted; continue without it." |
| 8 | Outcomes | A hire that succeeds → `done` (`worker_id`, `fired_at`). A hire error → `failed` with the hire's message.
